L L -functions and linear periods for 𝐆𝐋 4 × 𝐆𝐋 2 \mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2} and 𝐆𝐔 2 , 2 × 𝐆𝐋 2 {\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
Antonio Cauchi, Armando Gutierrez Terradillos

Abstract
. We give a new integral representation of the ∧ 2 ⊗ std 2 \wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2} L L -function of generic cusp forms on 𝐆𝐔 2 , 2 × 𝐆𝐋 2 {\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2} and 𝐆𝐋 4 × 𝐆𝐋 2 \mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2} and we use it to prove a relation between its central L L -value and the non-spherical period over 𝐆𝐋 2 × det 𝐆𝐋 2 \mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2} . Exploiting the theta correspondence for ( 𝐆𝐋 4 , 𝐆𝐋 4 ) (\mathbf{GL}_{4},\mathbf{GL}_{4}) , we obtain a relation between the central value of the L L -function attached to the strongly tempered spherical pair ( 𝐆𝐋 4 × 𝐆𝐋 2 , 𝐆𝐋 2 × 𝐆𝐋 2 ) (\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2},\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}) and its associated period. In the case of cusp forms on 𝐆𝐋 4 × 𝐆𝐋 2 \mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2} that are unramified everywhere, our formula gives new evidence towards a conjecture of Wan–Zhang.



1.
Introduction

Periods of automorphic forms are ubiquitous in Number Theory and Arithmetic Geometry. The non-triviality of automorphic periods is often expected to detect phenomena of Langlands functoriality and the non-vanishing of special values of
L
-functions. Exploring this “paradigm” has opened rich avenues for arithmetic applications - for instance, the results
[
54
]
or
[
28
]
have played an important role in applications to the Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture. The first results and conjectures aiming at a systematic study of the relationship between automorphic periods and
L
-functions are
[
22
]
and
[
11
]
. They set out a conjectural framework where diagonal periods for orthogonal and unitary groups detect the non-vanishing of central standard
L
-values.

Recently, the works of Sakellaridis–Venkatesh
[
48
]
and Ben–Zvi–Sakellaridis–Venkatesh
[
2
]
proposed a relative Langlands duality in the context of spherical varieties and hyperspherical Hamiltonian spaces. These works led to a new fertile ground of conjectural formulae, which relate automorphic periods and
L
-functions associated to these spaces.
To this extent, the works of Wan–Zhang
[
55
]
and Mao–Wan–Zhang
[
40
]
, to cite a few, have shed light on various aspects of these phenomena.

We now recall the definition of a family of period integrals that naturally appear in these settings. Let
G
be a reductive group defined over a number field
F
, and
H
a closed connected subgroup of
G
.
Let
A_{G}
be the maximal split subtorus of the center of
G
and
A_{G,H}=A_{G}\cap H
. For a cusp form
\varphi
on
G({\bf A}_{F})
with central character trivial on
A_{G,H}
, we define the period of
\varphi
over
H
to be

\mathcal{P}_{H}(\varphi):=\int_{A_{G,H}({\bf A}_{F})H(F)\setminus H({\bf A}_{F})}\varphi(h)\,dh.

In the notation of
[
40
]
, this is the period integral associated to the BZSV quadruple
\Delta=(G,H,0,1)
.
If
\pi
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G({\bf A}_{F})
, then the restriction of
\mathcal{P}_{H}
to
\pi
defines a functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{H}|_{\pi}\in{\rm Hom}_{H({\bf A}_{F})}(\pi,\mathbf{1}).

If
\mathcal{P}_{H}|_{\pi}
is not identically zero,
\pi
is said to be
H
-distinguished. One of the most fundamental problems in the relative Langlands program is to establish the relation between
\mathcal{P}_{H}|_{\pi}
and special values of a certain automorphic
L
-function
L(s,\pi,\rho_{X})
of
\pi
, attached to the
G
-variety
X=H\backslash G
(or, rather, to the BZSV quadruple dual to
\Delta
). When
X=H\backslash G
is a spherical
G
-variety (
i.e.
a Borel subgroup of
G
has a dense orbit in
X
), Sakellaridis showed that
L(s,\pi,\rho_{X})
is determined by the colors of the spherical variety
X
, see
[
49
]
.

1.1.
Main Theorems

Let
F
be a number field and let us denote by
{\bf A}
its adele ring. Fix
E/F
a quadratic field extension of
F
defining the quasi-split unitary group
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
. In this manuscript, the ambient group
G
is equal to either
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
or
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
.
Let
\pi\otimes\sigma
be a globally generic cuspidal automorphic representation of
G
, with central character satisfying
\omega_{\pi}|_{{\bf A}^{\times}}\,\omega_{\sigma}=1
, and consider their
\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}

L
-function. Using the exceptional isomorphisms to general Spin groups, the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for
({\mathbf{SO}}_{6},{\mathbf{SO}}_{3})
(see
[
11
]
) predicts that the non-vanishing of
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
is detected by the non-triviality of certain generalized Shalika periods for
G
and its pure inner forms (see Definition
7.29
, where these periods are introduced in the case of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
).

To study this
L
-value, we introduce a new integral representation of this
L
-function, which we describe in §
1.2
. In Theorem
6.9
, using this and the Siegel–Weil formula, we obtain a period relation between the central (partial)
L
-value
L^{S}(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
and the automorphic period
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}
. Here,
\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}
is embedded into
G
via the composition of the map

\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}\times_{\nu,{\rm det}}\mathbf{GL}_{2},

given in (
38
) together with the natural embedding of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}
inside either
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
or
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
. A special instance of Theorem
6.9
takes the following shape (see Corollary
6.11
).

Theorem A
.

Let
\pi
and
\sigma
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
respectively, which are unramified everywhere and such that the product of their central characters is trivial. Then,

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0\Longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0,

where
L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
denotes the completed
L
-function.

Surprisingly, in Theorem
A
(and Theorem
6.9
), we relate the central
\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}

L
-value to a
non-spherical
period, in the sense that
(G,\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2})
is not a spherical pair. Even if not spherical, the period associated to
(G,\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2})
should have several arithmetic applications, since it has a cohomological interpretation. By the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture and our results, this period should be intimately related to the generalized Shalika period, which does not directly appear in the cohomology of locally symmetric spaces. Considerations on the relation between linear periods and generalized Shalika models appear in
[
9
, §8]
.

We would like to mention that there are similar cases where both non-spherical but cohomological periods and generalized Shalika periods appear. For instance, Wan–Zhang in
[
55
]
discuss the relation between the central
{\rm Spin}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}

L
-value for
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
and generalized Shalika periods. However, using techniques similar to those of Theorem
A
, one can show that the non-vanishing of this central
L
-value is related to the period
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}}
. Details of this computation will appear elsewhere.

Until the end of the current subsection, we set
G
to be equal to
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
. Consider the embedding of algebraic groups

\displaystyle\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2},

\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},g_{2}),g_{2}\right),

where
\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},g_{2})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}&\\
&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. The quotient

\mathbf{X}:=(\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})\setminus\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2},

defines a strongly tempered spherical variety (see
[
55
, Table 1]
). Associated with
\mathbf{X}
, there is a representation
\rho_{\mathbf{X}}
of
{}^{L}(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})
, given by

\rho_{\mathbf{X}}:=\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}\oplus{\rm std}_{4}\oplus{\rm std}_{4}^{\vee},

where
{\rm std}_{4}
and
{\rm std}_{4}^{\vee}
denote the standard representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf C})
and its dual, respectively. Building on
[
48
]
, the works
[
55
]
and
[
44
]
computed the local relative characters for the pair
(\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v}),\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v}))
at unramified places
v
(with
v
a place of
F
) and determined the local multiplicities of the local avatars of the automorphic period
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}
. Motivated by these results, in
[
55
]
Wan--Zhang made
1
1

1

The authors in
[
55
]
formulated weak global conjectures as well as their Ichino–Ikeda type refinements for 10 strongly tempered spherical varieties, with
\mathbf{X}
one of them.
a
weak global conjecture
, which roughly takes the following form.

Conjecture 1.1

(
[
55
, Conjecture 1.9]
,
[
48
, §17]
)
.

Let
\pi
and
\sigma
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
, respectively, with trivial product of central characters. The following are equivalent:

(1)

L(\frac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0
;

(2)

There exists a quaternion algebra
D/F
such that the period integral
\mathcal{P}_{D^{\times}\times D^{\times}}|_{\pi_{D}\otimes\sigma_{D}}
is not identically zero, where
\pi_{D}
, resp.
\sigma_{D}
, are automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{2}(D)
, resp.
D^{\times}
, related to
\pi
, resp.
\sigma
, via the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence.

Moreover, if the above conditions hold, there exist a unique
D
for which Condition (2) holds.

In
[
44
]
, under certain technical conditions, it was proved that

(1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0\Longrightarrow L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0,

thus giving evidence towards one direction of Conjecture
1.1
in the case of
D
split.

The discrepancy between the results of Theorem
A
and (
1
) with Conjecture
1.1
seems to rely on the quotient

(2)

\displaystyle\frac{L(\frac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})}{L(\frac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})}=L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4}^{\vee}).

This ratio turns out to be non-zero if and only if
\pi
participates (non-trivially) in the theta correspondence for the dual pair
(\mathbf{GL}_{4},\mathbf{GL}_{4})
. One of the advantages of the use of the theta lifting is that it produces various period relations by using combinations of seesaw identities and Siegel–Weil formulae. We exploit this principle to prove the following (see Corollaries
7.27
and
7.31
).

Theorem B
.

Let
\pi
and
\sigma
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
respectively, which are unramified everywhere and such that the product of their central characters is trivial. Then,

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

In particular, if we further assume that

(i)

\pi
and
\sigma
have trivial central characters,

(ii)

L(\frac{3}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0
, which is always the case if
\pi
and
\sigma
are tempered,

then,

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0\Longleftrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

In the second part of the statement, we assume hypotheses
(i)
and
(ii)
to ensure that (
1
) (
i.e.

[
44
, Corollary 1.3(6)]
) holds. We observe that, to further deduce the non-vanishing of
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})
from (
1
), one also needs a comparison between linear periods and generalized Shalika periods, which we discuss in Lemma
7.30
.

One of the crucial ingredients in the proof of Theorem
B
is the theta correspondence for
(\mathbf{GL}_{4},\mathbf{GL}_{4})
, which allows us to take into account the discrepancy (
2
). This is because
\pi
is in the image of this theta correspondence if and only if the central value of the completed
L
-function
L(z,\pi,{\rm std}_{4})
is non-zero. The explicit use of this theta correspondence allows us to “upgrade” our zeta integral to a two complex variable zeta integral in
s
and
z
, which calculates

L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})L(z,\pi,{\rm std}_{4}),

and let us connect the central
L
-value
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})
to the spherical period
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}
.

As for Theorem
A
, we obtain a period relation for the non-vanishing of the partial
L^{S}(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})
for representations which are unramified outside of a finite set
S
of places of
F
, provided that we have certain control on the local zeta integrals at places in
S
. We invite the reader to consult Corollary
7.27
for a precise statement. We give a sketch of the proofs of Theorems
A
and
B
in §
1.3
below.

We conclude this subsection with a few remarks outlining how Theorem
B
might be extended. Firstly, we expect that the full Conjecture
1.1
might be tackled using the zeta integrals that we introduce.
In order to do that, we would require an explicit formula of the Jacquet–Langlands lift of
\pi
to inner forms of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
in the style of the Shimizu–Jacquet–Langlands formula for
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
proved by Shimizu
[
50
]
(see Remark
7.13
for more details).

We also expect that the proof of Theorem
B
might be adapted to the case of
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
, but with substantial technical differences due to the dissimilarity between type
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{II}
dual reductive pairs. We hope to explore some of these points in the future.

1.2.
New integral representations

The starting point of our treatise is a new integral representation of the
\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}

L
-function on generic cusp forms on
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
.

Let
\pi
, resp.
\sigma
, be cuspidal automorphic representations of either
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
or
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
, resp.
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
. Building on the works
[
7
]
and
[
8
]
, where we studied the relation between automorphic periods and the value at
s=1
of the
\wedge^{2}

L
-function of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
, we consider

Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})=\int_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}(F)Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})\setminus{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!\!E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})\varphi(g)dg,

where
\varphi
is a cusp form in the space of
\pi
and
E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})
is a normalized non-degenerate Eisenstein series constructed from
\sigma
relative to the Klingen parabolic
P_{(1,2)}
. We can interpret
Z(s,\varphi,\phi_{s})
as a degenerate Gross–Prasad period for
(\mathbf{GSpin}_{6},\mathbf{GSpin}_{5})
. As shown in Proposition
3.2
, the integral unfolds to the Whittaker models of
\pi
and
\sigma
. Hence, using the Casselman–Shalika formula, we prove the following.

Theorem C

(Theorem
3.5
)
.

Let
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}
and
f_{s}=\otimes_{v}f_{s,v}
be pure tensors. Let
S
be a finite set of places of
F
containing the archimedean places and the ramified places for
\varphi
,
f_{s}
, and
E/F
(if working with
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
). Then we have

Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})=L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\cdot\prod_{v\in S}Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s}),

where
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s})
is the local zeta integral introduced in Corollary
3.3
.

In Theorem
4.1
, we calculate the local zeta integral at any archimedean place
v
of
F
in the case of unramified representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
, relating it to the archimedean
L
-factor
L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
. Here, we crucially employ the formulae of spherical Whittaker functions given by
[
30
]
and
[
31
]
. Our result implies that
Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})
computes the completed
\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}

L
-function of cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
, which are unramified everywhere. It is also worth mentioning here that, in Proposition
8.1
, we use
Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})
to deduce that
L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
can have at most a simple pole at
s=1
. Using a Siegel–Weil formula of
[
36
]
, we relate the existence of this pole to a certain automorphic period (Corollary
8.6
).

We would like to remark that, on its own, the zeta integral
Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})
might be used to address motivic and Iwasawa-theoretic questions. Indeed, since
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}
and
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
have Shimura varieties, our integral might lead to the construction of new Euler systems, built from certain cohomology classes in the Eisenstein cohomology of the Shimura variety of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}
. To this end, in
[
6
]
, we compute the archimedean zeta integral for generic discrete series on
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}({\bf R})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf R})
.

For the rest of the subsection,
\pi
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
. In
[
56
]
, the author considers a family of automorphic lifts

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{4}])\times\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{A}([\mathbf{GL}_{4}]),

\displaystyle(\varphi,\eta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi^{z}_{\eta},

depending on a complex parameter
z
, which is exactly the theta lift for
(\mathbf{GL}_{4},\mathbf{GL}_{4})
when
z=0
. For a fixed
z\in{\bf C}
, we let
\Theta^{z}(\pi)
be the space spanned by the automorphic forms
\varphi^{z}_{\eta}
, with
\varphi
in the space of
\pi
. Watanabe shows that
\varphi^{z}_{f}
is a (possibly zero) cusp form whenever
\mathrm{Re}(z)
is big enough (see
[
56
, p. 707]
). We thus define

J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta):=\int_{{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F)Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})\setminus{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!\!\!E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})\varphi_{\eta}^{z}(g)|{\rm det}(g)|_{\bf A}^{-z}dg,

when the integral converges absolutely. We remark that, when
\pi
is in the image of the theta correspondence from
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
, we have the equality

J(s,0,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)=Z(s,\varphi^{0}_{\eta},f_{s}).

Combining Theorem
7.4
and Lemma
7.5
, we prove the following.

Corollary D
.

Let
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}
,
\eta=\otimes_{v}\eta_{v}
, and
f_{s}=\otimes_{v}f_{s,v}
be pure tensors. Let
S
be a finite set of places of
F
containing the archimedean places and the ramified places for
\varphi
,
\eta
, and
f_{s}
. We have the equality

J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)=L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\prod_{v\in S}J^{\star}(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v}),

where
J^{\star}(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v})
denotes the local zeta integral introduced in Lemma
7.5
.

The proof of Corollary
D
follows from the local computations of Theorem
C
and the formulae of the Whittaker model of
\varphi^{z}_{\eta}
given in
[
56
]
.

1.3.
Sketch of the proofs of Theorems
A
and
B

These proofs extend the methods developed in
[
54
]
,
[
23
]
, and
[
20
]
. From now on, we assume that
\pi
and
\sigma
are cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
, which are unramified everywhere and such that the product of their central characters is trivial. Although the arguments and results in the manuscript apply more generally, these assumptions simplify the exposition while preserving the essential ideas.

The strategy is summarized by the following diagram. Solid arrows represent equivariant maps between non-trivial representations. Dotted arrows, instead, either represent
{\bf C}
-valued functionals or indicate that the corresponding functional unfolds to the period that the arrow points to.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P_{(3,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1})\otimes\sigma\otimes\Theta^{z}(\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})\otimes\Theta^{z}(\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{6})\otimes\sigma\otimes\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{Q_{(2,4)}}(1/2,\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)\otimes\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P_{(3,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1})\otimes\sigma\otimes\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})\otimes\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P_{(2,2)}}(1/2,\mathbf{1})\otimes\sigma\otimes\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(1/2\text{,}\pi\text{,}\mathrm{std}_{4})\neq 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z=0\text{ }}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{Garrett's pullback formula})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{(seesaw)}\circ\text{(Siegel-Weil)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}s=1/2\\
z=0\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(s\text{,}z\text{,-,-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}\text{(Orthogonal pullback formula)}\\
\circ\\
\text{(Siegel-Weil \& Shimizu-Jacquet-Langlands)}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{1}\setminus\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}]}}
(Siegel-Weil)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s=1/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{Garrett's pullback formula})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(s\text{,-,-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GSp}_{6}}({\bf A})\setminus[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GSp}_{4}]}}

Here, the
I_{P_{(m,2(n-m))}}(-)
’s denote the induced representations of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}({\bf A})
defined in (
5
), (
25
) and (
35
), and
I_{Q_{(2,4)}}(s,\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)
denotes the induced representation of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}({\bf A})
defined in (
60
).

The proof of Theorem
A
follows from the
bottom floor
of the diagram, while the
higher floor
will lead to Theorem
B
, as we now explain. Start with factorizable data

\phi_{s}\otimes\Psi\otimes\varphi\in I_{P_{(3,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1})\otimes\sigma\otimes\pi,

and assume that
\phi_{1/2}
lies in the image of the theta correspondence of the trivial representation of
\mathbf{O}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
for the dual reductive pair
(\mathbf{O}(\mathbb{H}^{2}),{\mathbf{Sp}}_{6})
. Associated to
\phi_{s}
, one can define the (normalized) degenerate Siegel Eisenstein series
E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(g,s,\phi_{s})
on
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}
. One can then consider the integral

Z^{\prime}(s,\varphi,\Psi,\phi_{s}):=\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{{\rm det},\nu}{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}]}E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(\iota(g_{1},g_{2}),s,\phi_{s})\Psi(g_{1})\varphi(g_{2})\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2},

where we have fixed an embedding
\iota:\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{{\rm det},\nu}{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}
. In §
5.1
, we extend Garrett’s pullback formula to the case of similitude groups to produce a
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}({\bf A})
-equivariant map

p_{\Psi}:I_{P_{(3,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1})\otimes\sigma\to I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi}),

which let us show, in Corollary
5.3
, that

Z^{\prime}(s,\varphi,\Psi,\phi_{s})=Z(s,\varphi,p_{\Psi}(\phi_{s})).

On the one hand, thanks to Theorem
C
, these integrals represent the
\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}

L
-function on
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
. On the other hand, in §
6.1
we adapt the (intermediate) Siegel–Weil formula of
[
36
, Theorem 6.12]
to the case of similitude groups. Concretely, we construct a section
F_{s}(\phi_{s})\in I_{P_{(2,2)}}(s,\mathbf{1})
such that

E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(g,1/2,\phi_{1/2})\doteq\mathrm{Res}_{s=1/2}E_{P_{(2,2)}}(g,s,F_{s}(\phi_{s})),

where
\doteq
means
=
up to an explicit non-trivial constant and where
E_{P_{(2,2)}}(g,s,F_{s}(\phi_{s}))
is a degenerate Eisenstein series on
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}
associated to the maximal parabolic
P_{(2,2)}
. Via an unfolding computation, we use this equality to prove that if
Z^{\prime}(1/2,\varphi,\Psi,\phi_{1/2})
is non-zero, the
non-spherical
period
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times_{\rm det}\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}
is not identically zero (see Theorem
6.9
and Corollary
6.11
). Combining these facts, we obtain Theorem
A
.

In order to access the
spherical
period associated to the spherical pair
(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2},\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})
, we use the two-variable integral
J(s,z,\varphi,\Psi,\phi_{s},\eta)
. Recall that, thanks to Corollary
D
, it computes the product of completed
L
-functions

L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4})
.

In §
7.3
, we establish a seesaw identity associated to the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}.}

In Proposition
7.11
, we use this and the regularized Siegel–Weil formula of
[
36
]
to write
J(1/2,0,\varphi,\Psi,\phi_{1/2},\eta)
as an integral involving the theta function for the dual reductive pair
(\mathbf{O}(\mathbb{H}^{6}),{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4})
and the integral studied by Shimizu. At this point, we use Shimizu–Jacquet–Langlands
[
50
]
and the Siegel–Weil formula proved by
[
57
]
to express
J(1/2,0,\cdots)
as a zeta integral, which involves the degenerate Siegel Eisenstein series on
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}
. As a last technical ingredient, in §
7.6
, we prove a pullback formula for Eisenstein series on orthogonal groups, relating the degenerate Siegel Eisenstein series on
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}
to a non-degenerate Eisenstein series on
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}
, constructed from
\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma
. Via an unfolding process, we realize the spherical period
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}
as an inner integral of
J(1/2,0,\cdots)
. This and Corollary
D
let us show that

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0,

concluding the proof of Theorem
B
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

•

F
denotes a number field, with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, and denote by
{\bf A}
its adeles. Let
E/F
be a quadratic field extension, which we write
E=F(\delta)
, and let
\bar{\bullet}
denote the non-trivial automorphism of order 2 of
E/F
.

•

We let
\zeta_{F}
denote the Dedekind zeta function of
F
. When
v
is a finite place of
F
, we let
F_{v}
be the
v
-adic completion of
F
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{v}
; we let
q_{v}
be the size of the quotient of
\mathcal{O}_{v}
modulo its maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
and fix a uniformizer
\varpi_{v}
of
\mathcal{O}_{v}
. Accordingly, we normalize the norm
|\cdot|
of
F_{v}
so that
|\varpi_{v}|=q_{v}^{-1}
.

•

We fix a non-trivial additive character
\psi:F\backslash{\bf A}\to{\bf C}
.

•

We let
I_{n}^{\prime}
be the
n\times n
anti-diagonal matrix with all entries 1 and denote

J_{2n}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&I_{n}^{\prime}\\
-I_{n}^{\prime}&\end{smallmatrix}\right).

•

For a reductive group
G
over
F
, let
\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([G])
denote the space of cuspidal automorphic forms on the automorphic quotient
[G]=G(F)\backslash G({\bf A})
.

•

The symbol
{\rm Ind}_{*}^{\bullet}(-)
denotes normalized induction from
*
to
\bullet
of
-
.

•

Given two reductive groups
G_{1},G_{2}\in\{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n},{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V},{\mathbf{GO}}_{V},{\mathbf{GU}}_{n,n}\}
with similitude characters
\nu_{i}:G_{i}\to\mathbf{GL}_{1}
, we denote by
G_{1}\boxtimes G_{2}
the fiber product
G_{1}\times_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}G_{2}
, i.e.

G_{1}\boxtimes G_{2}=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\,:\,\nu_{1}(g_{1})=\nu_{2}(g_{2})\}.

2.2.
Symplectic and Unitary Groups

For every positive integer
n
, define
{\mathbf{GU}}_{n,n}
the group scheme over
F
given by

{\mathbf{GU}}_{n,n}(R)=\{(g,m_{g})\in\mathbf{GL}_{2n,F}(R\otimes_{F}E)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1,F}(R):{}^{t}\bar{g}J_{2n}g=m_{g}J_{2n}\},

where
R
denotes any
F
-algebra. Denote by
\nu:{\mathbf{GU}}_{n,n}\to\mathbf{GL}_{1,F},\;g\mapsto m_{g}
the similitude character. Inside
{\mathbf{GU}}_{n,n}
, we have the subgroup

{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}(R)=\{(g,m_{g})\in\mathbf{GL}_{2n,F}(R)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1,F}(R):{}^{t}gJ_{2n}g=m_{g}J_{2n}\},

which embeds into
{\mathbf{GU}}_{n,n}
via the natural inclusion. When
n=1
,
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2}=\mathbf{GL}_{2}
. We again denote by
\nu:{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}\to\mathbf{GL}_{1,F},\;g\mapsto m_{g}
the similitude character. The kernel of
\nu
is denoted by
{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}
.

Remark 2.1
.

These groups arise as the generic fiber of groups schemes over
\mathcal{O}
by replacing
E
and
F
by
\mathcal{O}_{E}
and
\mathcal{O}
in the definition. For any such group
G
, we denote by
G(\mathcal{O})
the group of
\mathcal{O}
-points of the resulting scheme. This choice fixes the maximal compact subgroups that will be used along the paper.

For any group
G\in\{\mathbf{GL}_{n},{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n},{\mathbf{GU}}_{n,n}\}
, we let
B_{G}=T_{G}\,U_{G}
be the upper triangular Borel subgroup of
G
, with
T_{G}
the maximal diagonal torus in
G
.

2.2.1.
Maximal parabolic subgroups

We denote by
W_{2n}
the standard representation of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}
with basis

\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n},f_{n},\dots,f_{2},f_{1}\}.

Then the subspace
W_{2n}^{+}
generated by
\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n}\}
is a maximal isotropic subspace of
W_{2n}
; we also let
W_{2n}^{-}:=\langle f_{n},\dots,f_{2},f_{1}\rangle
, so that

W_{2n}=W_{2n}^{+}\oplus W_{2n}^{-}.

For any
0\leq m\leq n
, we let
P_{(m,2(n-m))}=M_{(m,2(n-m))}\,U_{(m,2(n-m))}
denote the maximal parabolic subgroup of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}
that stabilizes the isotropic subspace
\langle e_{1},\cdots,e_{m}\rangle\subseteq W_{2n}^{+}.
The parabolic subgroup
P_{(m,2(n-m))}
has Levi

M_{(m,2(n-m))}\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{m}\times{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2(n-m)},

where we write
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{0}=\mathbf{GL}_{1}
. In particular,
P_{(n,0)}
is the Siegel parabolic of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}
. We then denote
P_{(m,2(n-m))}^{\circ}=M_{(m,2(n-m))}^{\circ}\,U_{(m,2(n-m))}
the intersection of
P_{(m,2(n-m))}
with
{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}
.

2.3.
Orthogonal groups

We denote by
\mathbb{H}
the hyperbolic plane,
i.e.
the
2
-dimensional quadratic
F
-vector space with quadratic form given by
I^{\prime}_{2}
.
Let
V
be any non-degenerate quadratic space over
F
of dimension
m
with symmetric bilinear form
B
. Then,
V
has an orthogonal decomposition of the form

V_{0}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{r},

with
V_{0}
a non-degenerate anisotropic quadratic space. The number
r
is called the Witt index of
V
. We refer to
V
being split if
r=\lfloor\tfrac{m}{2}\rfloor
.

We let
\mathbf{O}_{V}
, resp.
\mathbf{SO}_{V}
, be the isometry group of
(V,B)
, resp. the subgroup of

\mathbf{O}_{V}
of determinant one matrices. We further define the orthogonal similitude group of
(V,B)
by the rule

\mathbf{GO}_{V}(R):=\{(g,\nu_{g}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{GL}_{V}(R)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(R)\,:\,B(gx,gy)=\nu_{g}^{\prime}B(x,y)\},

where
R
denotes any
F
-algebra. When
m
is even, we define the subgroup

\mathbf{GSO}_{V}(R):=\{(g,\nu_{g}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{GO}_{V}(R)\,:\,{\rm det}(g)=(\nu_{g}^{\prime})^{m/2}\}.

We call
\nu^{\prime}:\mathbf{GO}_{V}\to\mathbf{GL}_{1},\,g\to\nu_{g}^{\prime}
the similitude character of
\mathbf{GO}_{V}
.

Remark 2.2
.

When
V
is four dimensional, we have a short exact sequence

1\to\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\to\mathbf{GO}_{V}\to\mu_{2}\to 1,

with
\mu_{2}=\{+,-\}
.

2.3.1.
Maximal parabolic subgroups

Let
V
be any non-degenerate quadratic space over
F
of dimension
m
and Witt index
r
. Then
V
has a maximal isotropic subspace
V^{+}
of dimension
r
and we can write

V=V^{+}\oplus V_{0}\oplus V^{-},

with
V^{-}
dual to
V^{+}
with respect to the quadratic form on
V
. Fix an ordered basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
of
V^{+}
and corresponding dual basis
\{f_{r},\dots,f_{1}\}
of
V^{-}
such that the restriction of the bilinear form
B
to
V^{+}\oplus V^{-}
is given by the matrix
2
2

2

This choice let us identify
V^{+}
and
V^{-}
with
(\mathbb{H}^{+})^{r}
and
(\mathbb{H}^{-})^{r}
, respectively.

I^{\prime}_{2r}
.

We then let, for any
0\leq a\leq r
,
Q_{(a,m-2a)}=L_{(a,m-2a)}\,N_{(a,m-2a)}
denote the maximal parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{GSO}_{V}
stabilizing the isotropic subspace
\langle e_{1},\dots,e_{a}\rangle\subseteq V^{+}
. Its Levi is

L_{(a,m-2a)}\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{a}\times\mathbf{GSO}_{V^{\prime}},

with
V^{\prime}=V_{0}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{r-a}
. Here, we use the convention
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\emptyset}:=\mathbf{GL}_{1}
. When
m=2r
and
a=r
, we refer to
Q_{(r,0)}
as the Siegel parabolic of
\mathbf{GSO}_{V}
. Finally, we denote by
Q^{\circ}_{(a,m-a)}=L^{\circ}_{(a,m-2a)}\,N_{(a,m-2a)}
the intersection of
Q_{(a,m-a)}
with
{\mathbf{SO}}_{V}
.

Remark 2.3
.

For instance, when
r=2
and
V=\mathbb{H}^{2}
with quadratic form associated with
I^{\prime}_{4}
, then
Q_{(2,0)}=L_{(2,0)}\,N_{(2,0)}
is such that

L_{(2,0)}=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g&\\
&\mu I_{2}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}g^{-1}I_{2}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\,\,g\in\mathbf{GL}_{2},\,\mu\in\mathbf{GL}_{1}\right\},

N_{(2,0)}=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&x&\\
&1&&-x\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\,\,x\in\mathbb{G}_{a}\right\}.

2.3.2.
Quaternionic quadratic spaces

Let
D
be a quaternion algebra over
F
with involution
\iota
and reduced norm
{\rm Nm}_{D}
. Consider
V=D
to be the quadratic space with non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form
B(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}_{D}(xy^{\iota})
for
x,y\in D
. Note that
B(x,x)={\rm Nm}_{D}(x)
for any
x\in D
. We have an
exceptional isomorphism

1\to\mathbf{GL}_{1}\xrightarrow{z}D^{\times}\times D^{\times}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathbf{GSO}_{D}\to 1,

where
z(t)=(t,t)
, for
t\in\mathbf{GL}_{1}
seen as an element in the center of
D^{\times}
, and
\rho(g,h)v=g^{-1}vh
for
g,h\in D^{\times}
and
v\in D
. Note that

\nu^{\prime}(\rho(g,h))={\rm Nm}_{D}(g)^{-1}{\rm Nm}_{D}(h).

We explicit the map
\rho
in the case where
D
is the split quaternion algebra
M_{2}(F)
, in which case the exceptional isomorphism reads as

(\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})/\{(z,z),\;z\in Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}}\}\simeq{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}.

The isomorphism is realized by identifying
\mathbb{H}^{2}\simeq M_{2}(F)
, with quadratic form
B(x,x)=\mathrm{det}(x)
. Let
e_{ij}\in M_{2}(F)
be the matrix with entry 1 at position
(i,j)
and
0
elsewhere. Fix the orthogonal basis
\{e_{11},e_{12},-e_{21},e_{22}\}
, so that we identify
M_{2}(F)\simeq F^{4}
, with bilinear form given by the matrix
I_{4}^{\prime}
.
Here, we think of
F^{4}
as row vectors and the
\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
-action is the usual right action. With these choices,
\rho:\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}\to\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
reads as

(3)

\displaystyle\rho:\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\mapsto\tfrac{1}{ad-bc}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}dp&dq&cp&-cq\\
dr&ds&cr&-cs\\
bp&bq&ap&-aq\\
-br&-bs&-ar&as\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Remark 2.4
.

Note that
\rho^{-1}Q_{(2,0)}
, with
Q_{(2,0)}
the Siegel parabolic of
\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
, corresponds to the parabolic subgroup
(B_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}}^{\rm opp}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})/\{(z,z)\}
of
(\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})/\{(z,z)\}
. Indeed, with the identifications of Remark
2.3
, we have

\rho\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\\
&{\mu}^{-1}\cdot{\rm det}(g)\end{smallmatrix}\right),g\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g&\\
&\mu I_{2}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}g^{-1}I_{2}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\,\,\rho\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\\
x&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),I_{2}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&x&\\
&1&&-x\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right).

2.4.
The Weil representation for similitude groups

Consider the dual pair
(\mathbf{O}_{V},{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n})
, with
V
a non-degenerate quadratic space of even dimension
m
. The vector space
\mathbb{W}=V\otimes_{F}W_{2n}
has a natural non-degenerate symplectic form and we fix the embedding
\mathbf{O}_{V}\times{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}\to{\mathbf{Sp}}_{\mathbb{W}}\simeq{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2nm}
, defined by

(v\otimes w)(h,g)=h^{-1}v\otimes wg,\,\text{for }v\in V,w\in W_{2n},h\in\mathbf{O}_{V},g\in{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}.

Fix a Witt decomposition
\mathbb{W}=\mathbb{W}^{+}\oplus\mathbb{W}^{-}
and let
\omega=\omega_{\psi}
be the
\mathrm{Schr\ddot{o}dinger}
model of the Weil representation of
{\mathbf{Sp}}_{\mathbb{W}}
corresponding to this polarization, which we realize as acting on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{W}^{+}({\bf A}))
. We then let
\omega
denote also its restriction to
\mathbf{O}_{V}\times{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}
. For further details, see, for instance,
[
45
]
.
We now let

R=\{(h,g)\in\mathbf{GO}_{V}\times{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}\,:\,\nu^{\prime}(h)=\nu(g)\}.

Contrary to
\nu
, the orthogonal similitude
\nu^{\prime}
is not always surjective. Moreover, there is an isomorphism
R\to{\mathbf{GO}}_{V}\ltimes{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n},(h,g)\mapsto(h,\tilde{g})=(h,d(\nu(g)^{-1})g),
where

d(x)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{n}&\\
&xI_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}.

The action of
R
on
V\otimes_{F}W_{2n}
induces an an embedding
\iota:R\to{\mathbf{Sp}}_{\mathbb{W}}
. Thus, the Weil representation extends naturally to
R
and hence to
{\mathbf{GO}}_{V}\ltimes{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}
, as

\omega(h,g)\varphi(x):=|\nu^{\prime}(h)|^{-\frac{n\mathrm{dim}(V)}{4}}\omega(\iota(h,g))\varphi(x).

See
[
47
, §3]
for further details. For instance, if we fix the polarization associated with
\mathbb{W}^{+}=V\otimes W_{2n}^{+}
, the extension of the Weil representation to
R
is explicitly given as follows. We let the group
{\mathbf{GO}}_{V}({\bf A})
act on
\mathcal{S}((V\otimes W_{2n}^{+})({\bf A}))
by

L(h)\phi(x\otimes y)=|\nu^{\prime}(h)|^{-\frac{n\cdot\dim(V)}{4}}\cdot\phi(h^{-1}x\otimes y).

Since, for
g\in{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}({\bf A})
, by
[
47
, Lemma 3.2]
one has

L(h)\omega(g)L(h^{-1})=\omega\left(d(\nu^{\prime}(h))gd(\nu^{\prime}(h)^{-1})\right),

we obtain a representation of
R({\bf A})
on
\mathcal{S}((V\otimes W_{2n}^{+})({\bf A}))
given by

(4)

\displaystyle\omega(h,g)\phi(x\otimes y)=(L(h)\omega(\tilde{g})\phi)(x\otimes y)=|\nu^{\prime}(h)|^{-\frac{n\cdot\dim(V)}{4}}\cdot(\omega(\tilde{g})\phi)(h^{-1}x\otimes y).

2.5.
Dual groups and the
\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}

L
-function

Recall that the dual group of
\mathbf{GL}_{n}
is
{}^{L}\mathbf{GL}_{n}=\mathbf{GL}_{n}({\bf C})
. We then let

{\rm std}_{n}
and
{\rm std}_{n}^{\vee}
denote the standard representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{n}({\bf C})
and its dual, respectively.

2.5.1.
Exterior square representations

The representation
{\rm std}_{4}
of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf C})
on
{\bf C}^{4}
, with basis
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\}
, induces the representation
\wedge^{2}
on
\wedge^{2}{\bf C}^{4}\simeq{\bf C}^{6}
by the rule
(\wedge^{2}g)\cdot(e_{i}\wedge e_{j})=g\cdot e_{i}\wedge g\cdot e_{j}
. This extends to a representation of the dual group of
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
, as follows.
Recall that is

{}^{L}{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}=(\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}({\bf C}))\rtimes{\rm Gal}(E/F),

with the action of the non-trivial element
\tau\in{\rm Gal}(E/F)
given by (cf.
[
3
, §1.8(c)]
)

(g,\lambda)\mapsto(\Phi_{4}{}^{t}g^{-1}\Phi_{4},\lambda{\rm det}(g)),\text{ where }\Phi_{4}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&&1\\
&&-1&\\
&1&&\\
-1&&&\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Consider the six dimensional representation

\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}({\bf C})\to\mathbf{GL}_{6}({\bf C}),

given by
(g,\lambda)\mapsto(\wedge^{2}g)\lambda
. By
[
33
, Lemma 2.1]
,
\wedge^{2}
extends to a representation of
{}^{L}{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
. Indeed, notice that both representations
\wedge^{2}
and
\wedge^{2}\circ\tau
, with
1\neq\tau\in{\rm Gal}(E/F)
, have same highest weight. Hence they are isomorphic,
i.e.
, there exists
A\in\mathbf{GL}_{6}({\bf C})
such that
(\wedge^{2}\circ\tau)(g,\lambda)=A^{-1}(\wedge^{2}(g,\lambda))A
. Choose
A
with
{\rm Tr}(A)>0
and extend
\wedge^{2}
to
{}^{L}{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
by sending

(g,\lambda,1)\mapsto(\wedge^{2}g)\lambda,\,(1,1,\tau)\mapsto A.

The resulting representation, which we still denote by
\wedge^{2}:{}^{L}{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}\to\mathbf{GL}_{6}({\bf C})
, is called the (twisted) exterior square representation of
{}^{L}{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
.

2.5.2.
L
-functions

Let
\mathbf{G}
be either
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
or
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
. Consider the tensor product

\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}:{}^{L}\mathbf{G}\;\times{}^{L}\mathbf{GL}_{2}\to\mathbf{GL}_{12}({\bf C}).

Let
\pi=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\pi_{v}
and
\sigma=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\sigma_{v}
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
, respectively. For any (possibly empty) finite subset
S
of places of
F
, we let

L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}):=\prod_{v\not\in S}L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}).

In particular, when
S=\emptyset
,
L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
denotes the completed
L
-function.

If
v
is a finite place of
F
, which is unramified for
\pi_{v}
,
\sigma_{v}
, and
E/F
(if
\mathbf{G}={\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
), then the local
L
-factor is given by

L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})=\mathrm{det}\left(1-(\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})(s_{\pi_{v}}\times s_{\sigma_{v}})\cdot q^{-s}\right)^{-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!,

where
s_{\pi_{v}}
and
s_{\sigma_{v}}
denote representatives of the Frobenius conjugacy classes of
\pi_{v}
and
\sigma_{v}
, respectively. Finally, we refer to §
4.3
, for a description of the local archimedean
L
-factors of the
\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}

L
-function of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
.

3.
The zeta integral

In this section, we let
\mathbf{H}={\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}
and let
\mathbf{G}
be either
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
or
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
. For
G\in\{\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}\}
, also let
B_{G}=T_{G}\,U_{G}
denote the upper triangular Borel subgroup of
G
, with
T_{G}
the maximal diagonal torus in
G
, and
U_{G}
the unipotent radical of
B_{G}
.

3.1.
The Klingen Eisenstein series

Fix a cuspidal automorphic representation
\sigma\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{2}])
with central character
\omega_{\sigma}
and a Hecke character
\chi
of
F^{\times}\backslash{\bf A}^{\times}
. For
s\in{\bf C}
, we let

(5)

I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\chi):={\rm Ind}_{P_{(1,2)}({\bf A})}^{\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\left(((\chi\omega_{\sigma})^{-1}\otimes{\sigma})\delta_{P_{(1,2)}}^{\tfrac{1}{2}(s-1/2)}\right)

be the space of smooth functions
f_{s}:{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}({\bf A})\to{\bf C}
such that, for every
n\in U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})
and
m=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&&\\
&r&\\
&&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{(1,2)}({\bf A})
, we have

f_{s}(nm)=(\chi\omega_{\sigma})^{-1}(d)\delta_{P_{(1,2)}}^{\tfrac{1}{2}(s+1/2)}(m)\Psi(r),

where
\Psi
is a cusp form in
\sigma
. Recall that the modulus character of
P_{(1,2)}
is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{P_{(1,2)}}

\displaystyle:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&&\\
&r&\\
&&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mapsto\left|\tfrac{a}{d}\right|^{2}.

This representation is a (possibly reducible) automorphic representation with central character
\chi^{-1}
.
Given a smooth section
f_{s}\in I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\chi)
, define

(6)

\displaystyle W_{\sigma,f_{s}}(g):=\int_{F\backslash{\bf A}}f_{s}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&&\\
&1&x&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)g\right)\psi(-x)dx,

which is an element of
I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\mathcal{W}_{\sigma},\chi)
, with
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}
the Whittaker model of
\sigma
. When the context is clear, we suppress
f_{s}
from the notation and denote this simply by
W_{\sigma,s}
. We have the Fourier expansion (
cf.

[
4
, (10.4)]
)

(7)

f_{s}(g)=\sum_{\alpha\in F^{\times}}W_{\sigma,s}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&&&\\
&\alpha&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)g\right).

Given a
K
-finite smooth section
f_{s}\in I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\chi)
, let
S
be a finite set of places containing the archimedean places of
F
and the places where
f_{s}
is ramified. Define the normalized Klingen Eisenstein series (cf.
[
4
, p.390]
)

(8)

\displaystyle E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s}):=L^{S}(2s,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\chi)\cdot\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\sum_{\gamma\in{P_{(1,2)}}(F)\backslash\mathbf{H}(F)}}f_{s}(\gamma g),

where
L^{S}(2s,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\chi)
is the partial symmetric square
L
-function
3
3

3

The calculation of the normalization factor follows from checking how the Satake parameters of
I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\chi)
at unramified primes act on
{}^{L}\mathfrak{n}_{{(1,2)}}
via the natural action of the Levi of
{}^{L}P_{(1,2)}
on it.
of
\sigma
twisted by
\chi
. Recall that, if
\sigma_{v}
is unramified with Frobenius conjugacy class of the form
\mathrm{diag}(\xi_{1}(\varpi_{v}),\xi_{2}(\varpi_{v}))
, then

(9)

L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\chi_{v})=(1-(\xi^{2}_{1}\chi_{v})(\varpi_{v})q_{v}^{-2s})(1-(\omega_{\sigma_{v}}\chi_{v})(\varpi_{v})q_{v}^{-2s})(1-(\xi^{2}_{2}\chi_{v})(\varpi_{v})q_{v}^{-2s}).

If
\mathrm{Re}(s)
is sufficiently large, the Eisenstein series defined by (
8
) converges absolutely and it extends meromorphically to a function on the whole complex plane. Note that the poles of the Eisenstein series
E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})
are given by the poles of the
L
-function
L^{S}(2s-1,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\chi)
. According to
[
18
, Theorem 9.3]
the twisted symmetric square
L
-function of
\sigma
can have poles only when
\sigma\otimes\eta\simeq\sigma
for some non-trivial quadratic character
\eta
. A detailed analysis of the possible isobaric factorization of the functorial lift
{\rm Sym}^{2}(\sigma)\otimes\chi
in this case reveals that a necessary condition for the pole is
\chi\omega_{\sigma}\neq 1
and
(\chi\omega_{\sigma})^{2}=1
.

3.1.1.
Spherical vectors

We assume that
f_{s}=\otimes_{v}f_{s,v}
is a pure tensor in
I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\chi)
. As in
[
4
, §3.10]
, to
f_{s}
we associate a function
W_{\sigma,s}
, which factors as a restricted product of elements

W_{\sigma_{v},s}\in{\rm Ind}_{P_{(1,2)}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{H}(F_{v})}\left(((\chi_{v}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})^{-1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}})\delta_{P_{(1,2)}}^{\tfrac{1}{2}(s-1/2)}\right),

with
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}}
the Whittaker model of
\sigma_{v}
. In particular, for
n\in U_{{(1,2)}}(F_{v})
,
m=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&&\\
&r&\\
&&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{(1,2)}(F_{v})
, and
g\in\mathbf{H}(F_{v})
, we have

W_{\sigma_{v},s}(nmg)=\delta_{P_{(1,2)}}^{\tfrac{1}{2}(s+1/2)}(m)(\chi_{v}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})^{-1}(d)W_{g,v}(r),

with
W_{g,v}\in\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}}
depending on
g
. When
v\not\in S
, we have a unique spherical vector
W_{s,v}^{\rm o}
, normalized so that its restriction to
\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
is one. Using the Iwasawa decomposition, write
g\in\mathbf{H}(F_{v})
as
nmk
, with
nm\in P_{(1,2)}(F_{v})
and
k\in\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
; then, if
m=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&&\\
&r&\\
&&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, we have

(10)

\displaystyle W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(nmk)=\left|\tfrac{a}{d}\right|^{s+1/2}(\chi_{v}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})^{-1}(d)W_{\sigma_{v}}(r),

with
W_{\sigma_{v}}
the spherical vector of
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}}
, normalized so that
W_{\sigma_{v}}
is equal to
1
on
\mathbf{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
(
cf.

[
4
, p. 393]
).

3.2.
The zeta integral and its unfolding

Consider
\pi\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{G}])
,
\sigma\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{2}])
cuspidal automorphic representations with central characters
\omega_{\pi}
and
\omega_{\sigma}
, respectively. Given a cusp form
\varphi\in\pi
and a factorizable section
f_{s}\in I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})
4
4

4

If
\mathbf{G}={\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
,
\omega_{\pi}
denotes its restriction
{\omega_{\pi|{\bf A}^{\times}}}
to
F\backslash{\bf A}^{\times}
, we define

Z(s,\varphi,f_{s}):=\int_{\mathbf{H}(F)Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!\!E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})\varphi(g)dg.

Since
\varphi|_{\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}
is rapidly decreasing and the Klingen Eisenstein series is of moderate growth and meromorphic, the integral converges absolutely and defines a meromorphic function on
s
. Let
\psi
be the non-trivial additive character of
F\backslash{\bf A}
fixed in §
2.1
.

Definition 3.1
.

•

If
\mathbf{G}={\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
, let
\chi:U_{\mathbf{G}}(F)\backslash U_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
be the principal character given

\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x&y&a\\
&1&b&\bar{y}\\
&&1&-\bar{x}\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\psi(\mathrm{Tr}_{E/F}(\delta x)-b).

•

If
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{GL}_{4}
, let
\chi:U_{\mathbf{G}}(F)\backslash U_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
be the principal character given

\chi\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&a&b&c\\
&1&d&e\\
&&1&f\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\psi(a-d+f).

We consider the Whittaker model of a cusp form
\varphi
of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to
\chi
,
i.e.

W_{\varphi}(g):=\int_{[U_{\mathbf{G}}]}\varphi(ng)\chi^{-1}(n)dn.

Proposition 3.2
.

The zeta integral unfolds to

Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})=L^{S}(2s,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi})\int_{U_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!\!\!W_{\sigma,s}\left(g\right)W_{\varphi}(g)dg.

Proof.

We prove the statement only in the case of
\mathbf{G}={\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
, as the computation when
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{GL}_{4}
is analogous. When
{\rm Re}(s)
is big enough, we can unfold the Eisenstein series and obtain

Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})=L^{S}(2s,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi})\int_{P_{(1,2)}(F)Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!f_{s}(g)\varphi(g)dg.

Using the invariance of
f_{s}(g)
over
U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})
, we collapse the sum over
U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})
to write the integral as

\int_{M_{(1,2)}(F)U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!f_{s}(g)\int_{[U_{(1,2)}]}\varphi(ng)dndg,

where
dn
is the Haar measure on
U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})
. Now, let
Q_{\mathbf{G}}=L_{\mathbf{G}}N_{\mathbf{G}}
be the standard Klingen parabolic of
\mathbf{G}
, with
L_{\mathbf{G}}\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{1}\times{\mathbf{GU}}_{1,1}
. The function
\varphi^{\prime}(g):=\int_{[U_{(1,2)}]}\varphi(ng)dn
is an automorphic form on
\mathbf{G}({\bf A})
invariant under
U_{(1,2)}
, hence we can Fourier expand it over
U_{(1,2)}\setminus N_{{\mathbf{G}}}
:

\varphi^{\prime}(g)=\sum_{\chi:[U_{(1,2)}\setminus N_{{\mathbf{G}}}]\to{\bf C}^{\times}}\varphi_{\chi}(g),

with
\varphi_{\chi}(g)=\int_{[N_{{\mathbf{G}}}]}\varphi(n^{\prime}g)\chi^{-1}(n^{\prime})dn^{\prime}
. Any character of
[U_{(1,2)}\setminus N_{{\mathbf{G}}}]
is of the form

\displaystyle\chi_{\alpha,\beta}:[U_{(1,2)}\setminus N_{{\mathbf{G}}}]

\displaystyle\to{\bf C}^{\times},

\displaystyle n^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto\psi({\rm Tr}_{E/F}(\alpha x+\beta y)),

with
\alpha,\beta\in E
such that
\bar{\alpha}=-\alpha
and
\bar{\beta}=-\beta
(
cf.

[
7
, Proposition 3.1]
). As in
loc.cit.
, we note that
M_{(1,2)}(F)
acts on the space of characters of
[U_{(1,2)}\setminus N_{{\mathbf{G}}}]
with two orbits, the trivial one and an open one. A representative of the open orbit is given by the character
\chi_{\delta,0}
. Since
\varphi
is a cusp form, the constant term
\varphi_{N_{{\mathbf{G}}}}(g)=0
, hence the integral can be written as

(11)

\int_{M_{\delta}(F)U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}f_{s}(g)\varphi_{\chi_{\delta,0}}(g)dg,

with

M_{\delta}(F)=L_{\delta}(F)N_{\delta}(F)=\left\{m(a,d)n(b)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&&&\\
&a&&\\
&&d&\\
&&&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&&\\
&1&b&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\;a,d\in F^{\times},\,b\in F\right\}.

Collapse (
11
) over
[N_{\delta}]
to obtain

\int_{L_{\delta}(F)N_{\delta}({\bf A})U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\int_{[N_{\delta}]}f_{s}(n(b)g)\varphi_{\chi_{\delta,0}}(n(b)g)dbdg.

By (
7
), we have

f_{s}(g)=\sum_{t\in F^{\times}}W_{\sigma,s}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t&&&\\
&t&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)g\right),

where
W_{\sigma,s}(g)
is the element of
I_{P_{(1,2)}}\left(s,\mathcal{W}_{\sigma},\omega_{\pi}\right)
defined in (
6
), with
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}
the Whittaker model of
\sigma
. Therefore the integral is equal to

(12)

\int_{L_{\delta}(F)U_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\int_{[N_{\delta}]}\sum_{t\in F^{\times}}W_{\sigma,s}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t&&&\\
&t&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)n(b)g\right)\varphi_{\chi_{\delta,0}}(n(b)g)dbdg.

Here, we used that
N_{\delta}({\bf A})U_{(1,2)}({\bf A})=U_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})
, with
U_{\mathbf{H}}
the unipotent radical of the upper triangular Borel of
\mathbf{H}
. Since
[N_{\delta}]
is compact and the sum (
7
) converges absolutely on compact sets, using Fubini’s theorem, the integral (
12
) is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{L_{\delta}(F)U_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in F^{\times}}\int_{[N_{\delta}]}W_{\sigma,s}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t&&&\\
&t&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)n(b)g\right)\varphi_{\chi_{\delta,0}}(n(b)g)dbdg

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}\int_{[N_{\delta}]}W_{\sigma,s}\left(n(b)g\right)\varphi_{\chi_{\delta,0}}(n(b)g)dbdg,

where we have used the equality
L_{\delta}(F)=Z_{\mathbf{H}}(F)\cdot\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t&&&\\
&t&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\;t\in F^{\times}\right\}
.
Finally, using the transformation property
W_{\sigma,s}(n(b)g)=\psi(b)W_{\sigma,s}(g)
, the above integral is equal to

\int_{U_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})Z_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf A})\setminus\mathbf{H}({\bf A})}W_{\sigma,s}\left(g\right)\int_{[N_{\delta}]}\varphi_{\chi_{\delta,0}}(n(b)g)\psi(b)dbdg.

The character on
[U_{\mathbf{G}}/[U_{\mathbf{G}},U_{\mathbf{G}}]]
, defined by taking
b\mapsto\psi(b)
on
N_{\delta}
and by taking
\chi_{\delta,0}
on the one parameter unipotent subgroup associated to the other simple root, is the principal character
\chi
of Definition
3.1
. The result thus follows.
∎

Using the uniqueness of Whittaker models, Proposition
3.2
has an immediate Corollary.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}
and
f_{s}=\otimes_{v}f_{s,v}
be factorizable elements in the space of
\pi
and
I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})
respectively. The zeta integral
Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})
admits the following Euler product expansion:

Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})=L^{S}(2s,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi})\prod_{v}Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s}),

where

Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s}):=\int_{U_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})Z_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})\setminus\mathbf{H}(F_{v})}W_{\sigma_{v},s}\left(g_{v}\right)W_{\pi_{v}}(g_{v})dg_{v}.

3.3.
Preparation to the unramified calculations

Let
v
be a finite place of
F
which is unramified for
\pi_{v}
,
\sigma_{v}
, and
E/F
. Recall that we have denoted by
q_{v}
the size of the quotient of
\mathcal{O}_{v}
modulo its maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
and fixed a uniformizer
\varpi_{v}
of
\mathcal{O}_{v}
such that the norm
|\cdot|
of
F_{v}
is normalized by setting
|\varpi_{v}|=q_{v}^{-1}
. Let
W_{\pi_{v}}
be the spherical vector in the Whittaker model of
\pi_{v}
, normalised so that it is equal to
1
on
\mathbf{G}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
. We also let
W_{\sigma_{v}}
be the spherical vector of
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}}
and let
W_{s,v}^{\rm o}
be the spherical vector of
{\rm Ind}_{P_{(1,2)}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{H}(F_{v})}\left(((\omega_{\pi_{v}}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})^{-1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}})\delta_{P_{(1,2)}}^{\tfrac{1}{2}(s-1/2)}\right)
, normalized as in (
10
). Using the Iwasawa decomposition
\mathbf{H}(F_{v})=B_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
, the local integral at
v
of Corollary
3.3
reads as

Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{s,v}^{\rm o})=L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})\int_{Z_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})\setminus T_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})}\delta_{B_{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}(t)W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(t)W_{\pi_{v}}(t)dt.

From now on, we identify
Z_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})\setminus T_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})
with
T_{1}(F_{v}):=\left\{t(ab,b;b)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}ab&&&\\
&b&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&a^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\;a,b\in F_{v}^{\times}\right\}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(t(ab,b;b))W_{\pi_{v}}(t(ab,b;b))\neq 0
, then
a,b\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
.

Proof.

We show the Lemma only when
\mathbf{G}={\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
, as the proof for the other case is almost identical. Write
u=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x&y&z\\
&1&&\overline{y}\\
&&1&-\overline{x}\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in U_{{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
and
u^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&&\\
&1&y^{\prime}&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in U_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
. By (
10
), we have that

\displaystyle W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(t(ab,b;b))

\displaystyle=W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(t(ab,b;b)u^{\prime})

\displaystyle=W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(t(ab,b;b)u^{\prime}t(ab,b;b)^{-1}t(ab,b;b))

\displaystyle=\psi_{v}(by^{\prime})W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(t(ab,b;b)).

If
W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(t(ab,b;b))\neq 0
, then
\psi_{v}(by^{\prime})=1
for every
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
. This implies that
b\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
. Similarly, since

\displaystyle W_{\pi_{v}}(t(ab,b;b))

\displaystyle=W_{\pi_{v}}(t(ab,b;b)u)=\psi_{v}({\rm Tr}_{(E\otimes_{F}F_{v})/F_{v}}(\delta_{v}ax))W_{\pi_{v}}(t(ab,b;b)),

if
W_{\pi_{v}}(t(ab,b;b))\neq 0
, then
a\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
. These two facts imply the desired result.
∎

Lemma
3.4
let us write
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{s,v}^{\rm o})
as

\displaystyle L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})\sum_{n,m\geq 0}\delta_{B_{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}(t(\varpi_{v}^{n+m},\varpi_{v}^{m};\varpi_{v}^{m}))W_{s,v}^{\rm o}\left(t(\varpi_{v}^{n+m},\varpi_{v}^{m};\varpi_{v}^{m})\right)W_{\pi_{v}}(t(\varpi_{v}^{n+m},\varpi_{v}^{m};\varpi_{v}^{m})).

By (
10
),

W_{s,v}^{\rm o}\left(t(\varpi_{v}^{n+m},\varpi_{v}^{m};\varpi_{v}^{m})\right)=q_{v}^{-(2n+m)(s+1/2)}(\omega_{\pi_{v}}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})(\varpi_{v}^{n})W_{\sigma_{v}}(\hat{t}(\varpi_{v}^{m},1)),

where
\hat{t}(\alpha,\beta):=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\\
&\beta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
. Using the action on the models by the center of
\mathbf{H}(F_{v})
and the equality
\delta_{B_{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}(t(\varpi_{v}^{n+m},\varpi_{v}^{m};\varpi_{v}^{m}))=q_{v}^{4n+3m}
,
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{s,v}^{\rm o})
is seen to be

(13)

\displaystyle L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})\sum_{n,m\geq 0}q_{v}^{4n+3m-(2n+m)(s+1/2)}W_{\sigma_{v}}(\hat{t}(\varpi_{v}^{n+m},\varpi_{v}^{n}))W_{\pi_{v}}(t(\varpi_{v}^{2n+m},\varpi_{v}^{n+m};\varpi_{v}^{2n+m})).

3.4.
Symmetric algebra factorizations and local unramified zeta integrals

We keep the notation of the previous section. In order to evaluate the local zeta integrals explicitly, we crucially use two key results appearing in the Appendix of
[
19
]
. With these in hand and some simple manipulation, we prove the following result.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}
and
f_{s}=\otimes_{v}f_{s,v}
be pure tensors in
\pi
and
I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})
, respectively. Let
S
be a finite set of places of
F
containing the archimedean places and the ramified places for
\varphi
,
f_{s}
, and
E/F
(if
\mathbf{G}={\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
). Then we have

Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})=L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\prod_{v\in S}Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s}).

Proof.

This is the combination of Corollary
3.3
with Corollaries
3.8
and
3.10
, which are proved below.
∎

The unramified computation for
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
will follow from the one of
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
at places which split in
E
. Therefore, in what follows we can assume that
\mathbf{G}={\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}
. Let
v\not\in S
. We distinguish the cases of
v
inert or split in
E
.

3.4.1.
The inert case

Let
\pi_{v}
and
\sigma_{v}
be unramified smooth representations of
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
. We have that
\pi_{v}
is the unique unramified subquotient of
\mathrm{Ind}_{B_{\mathbf{G}}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{G}(F_{v})}\chi
with
\chi
an unramified character of
T_{\mathbf{G}}(F_{v})
. Denote by
\pi_{v}^{\prime}
the unique unramified subquotient of
\mathrm{Ind}_{B_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{H}(F_{v})}\chi_{|_{T_{\mathbf{H}}}}
. Recall that the Casselman–Shalika formula for the Whittaker coefficient of
\pi_{v}
involves the Satake parameters
s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}
of
\pi_{v}^{\prime}
(
cf
.
[
13
, Proposition 5.2.1]
). It is thus natural to try to relate the integral (
13
) firstly to an
L
-factor for
\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v}
. Recall that the dual group of
\mathbf{H}
is isomorphic to itself. Let

\Lambda^{+}:=\{\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in{\bf Z}^{3},\,k_{1}\geq k_{2}\geq 0\}

be the set of dominant weights for
\mathbf{H}({\bf C})
and let
\varpi_{v}^{\mathbf{k}}=t(\varpi_{v}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}},\varpi_{v}^{k_{1}+k_{3}};\varpi_{v}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+2k_{3}})
. Then, the Casselman–Shalika formula (
cf
.
[
10
, (3.9)]
) gives that

(14)

\displaystyle W_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v}^{\mathbf{k}})\cdot\delta_{B_{\mathbf{G}}}^{-1/2}(\varpi_{v}^{\mathbf{k}})=W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}(\varpi_{v}^{\mathbf{k}})\cdot\delta_{B_{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1/2}(\varpi_{v}^{\mathbf{k}})={\rm Tr}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}|V_{\mathbf{k}}),

where
V_{\mathbf{k}}
is the irreducible representation of
\mathbf{H}({\bf C})
of highest weight
\mathbf{k}\in\Lambda^{+}
. Applying this formula to the infinite series of (
13
) we get back the local integral of
[
42
]
, which calculates
L(2s,\omega_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})L(s,\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v},r),
as the following Proposition shows. Here we have denoted
r:\mathbf{H}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})\to\mathbf{GL}_{8}({\bf C})
the tensor product of the two standard representations.

Proposition 3.6
.

We have that

Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{s,v}^{\rm o})=L(2s,\omega_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})^{-1}L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})L(s,\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v},r).

Proof.

The Casselman–Shalika formula for
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
says that

\displaystyle W_{\sigma_{v}}(\hat{t}(\varpi_{v}^{m+n},\varpi_{v}^{n}))

\displaystyle=q_{v}^{-m/2}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\sigma_{v}}|W_{(m+n,n)}),

where
W_{(m+n,n)}
is the irreducible representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
of highest weight
(m+n,n)
. On the other hand, (
14
) applied to
\mathbf{k}=(m+n,n,0)
gives that

\displaystyle W_{\pi_{v}}(t(\varpi_{v}^{2n+m},\varpi_{v}^{m+n};\varpi_{v}^{2n+m}))

\displaystyle=q_{v}^{-3n-2m}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}|V_{(m+n,n,0)}).

Plugging in these formulas, (
13
) is equal to

\displaystyle L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})\sum_{n,m\geq 0}q_{v}^{-s(2n+m)}\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}|V_{(m+n,n,0)}\otimes W_{(m+n,n)}).

The result then follows from Lemma
3.7
below.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

We have

L(s,\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v},r)=L(2s,\omega_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})\sum_{n,m\geq 0}q_{v}^{-s(2n+m)}\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}|V_{(m+n,n,0)}\otimes W_{(m+n,n)}),

where
V_{(m+n,n,0)}
, resp.
W_{(m+n,n)}
, is the irreducible representation of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}({\bf C})
, resp.
\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
, of highest weight
(m+n,n,0)
, resp.
(m+n,n)
.

Proof.

This follows from the calculation of the right hand side of
[
19
, (A.1.1)]
in
[
19
, Proof of Proposition A.1]
at pages 139-140 and we only sketch it. By the Poincaré polynomial identity
[
19
, (A.1.2)]
, we have

L(s,\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v},r)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{Sym}^{\ell}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}))q_{v}^{-s\ell},

where
\mathbf{Sym}^{\ell}
is the
\ell
-th symmetric power of
r
, one is left to describe the irreducible components of each representation
\mathbf{Sym}^{\ell}
. At page 140 of
loc.cit.
it is shown that
5
5

5

The formula at page 140 of
[
19
]
is given for the standard representation of
{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2n}\times\mathbf{GL}_{n}
, however it extends to the one of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}\times\mathbf{GL}_{n}
after taking into account that
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{2n}
acts on the algebra
I(E^{*})
of
loc.cit.
by the symplectic multiplier.

\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{Sym}^{\ell}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}))=\sum_{2i+j=\ell}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{Sym}^{i}(\nu(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}})\Lambda^{2}(s_{\sigma_{v}})))\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq 0\\
m_{1}+m_{2}=j\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}|V_{(m_{1},m_{2},0)}\otimes W_{(m_{1},m_{2})}),

where recall that
\nu:\mathbf{H}({\bf C})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
denotes the symplectic multiplier.
This and the Poincaré polynomial identity show that

L(s,\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v},r)=L(2s,\omega_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})\sum_{m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq 0}\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}|V_{(m_{1},m_{2},0)}\otimes W_{(m_{1},m_{2})})q_{v}^{-s(m_{1}+m_{2})},

as desired.
∎

We now relate the
L
-functions of
\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v}
and
\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v}
and obtain the following.

Corollary 3.8
.

We have the equality

Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{s,v}^{\rm o})=L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}).

Proof.

The semi-simple Frobenius conjugacy class of
\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})
in
\mathbf{GL}_{6}({\bf C})
can be represented by

\chi_{0}(\varpi_{v}){\rm diag}(1,\chi_{1}(\varpi_{v}),\chi_{2}(\varpi_{v}),\chi_{1}(\varpi_{v})\chi_{2}(\varpi_{v}),x,y),

with
x
and
y
the roots of
X^{2}-\chi_{1}(\varpi_{v})\chi_{2}(\varpi_{v})
(cf.
[
33
, p. 12]
), while the one of
\pi_{v}^{\prime}
can be chosen to be

\chi_{0}(\varpi_{v}){\rm diag}(1,\chi_{1}(\varpi_{v}),\chi_{2}(\varpi_{v}),\chi_{1}(\varpi_{v})\chi_{2}(\varpi_{v})).

Writing the semi-simple Frobenius conjugacy class of
s_{\sigma_{v}}
as
{\rm diag}(\xi_{1}(\varpi_{v}),\xi_{2}(\varpi_{v}))\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
, we then have that

\displaystyle\frac{L(s,\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma_{v},r)}{L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{2}(1-x\chi_{0}(\varpi_{v})\xi_{i}(\varpi_{v})q_{v}^{-s})(1-y\chi_{0}(\varpi_{v})\xi_{i}(\varpi_{v})q_{v}^{-s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{2}(1-(\xi_{i}^{2}\omega_{\pi_{v}})(\varpi_{v})q_{v}^{-2s})

\displaystyle=L(2s,\omega_{\sigma_{v}}\omega_{\pi_{v}})L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})^{-1},

where we used that
\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})=\omega_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}(\varpi_{v})=(\chi_{0}^{2}\chi_{1}\chi_{2})(\varpi_{v})
and the explicit formula (
9
). This and Proposition
3.6
give the result.
∎

3.4.2.
The split case

Let
v
be a split prime in
E
and let
\pi_{v}
and
\sigma_{v}
be unramified representations of
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
respectively. The isomorphism
E\otimes_{F}F_{v}\simeq F_{v}\times F_{v}
induces an isomorphism
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(E\otimes_{F}F_{v})\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})
. If
g
maps to
(g_{1},g_{2})
, then
\bar{g}
is sent to
(g_{2},g_{1})
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}(F_{v})\simeq\{(g_{1},g_{2},m)\in\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F_{v})\,:\,g_{2}=mJ{}^{t}g_{1}^{-1}J^{-1}\},

and then, projection to the first factor induces an isomorphism
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F_{v}),\,(g_{1},g_{2},m)\mapsto(g_{1},m)
. This allows us to regard
\pi_{v}
as a representation
\pi_{v}^{\prime}\otimes\chi_{v}
of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F_{v})
. Furthermore, the restriction to
F_{v}^{\times}
of the central character
\omega_{\pi_{v}}
is identified with
\omega_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\chi_{v}^{2}
.

Let
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\}
denote a basis of the standard representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf C})
and define the symmetric form
Q
on
\wedge^{2}{\bf C}^{4}
by setting

e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\wedge e_{k}\wedge e_{\ell}=Q(e_{i}\wedge e_{j},e_{k}\wedge e_{\ell})e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}\wedge e_{4}

and then extending it by linearity. With respect to the basis

\{e_{1}\wedge e_{2},e_{1}\wedge e_{3},e_{1}\wedge e_{4},e_{2}\wedge e_{3},-e_{2}\wedge e_{4},e_{3}\wedge e_{4}\},

the symmetric form
Q
is given by the matrix
I^{\prime}_{6}
. Hence, there are isomorphisms
{\mathbf{GO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\simeq{\mathbf{GO}}_{\wedge^{2}{\bf C}^{4}}({\bf C})
as well as
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\simeq{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\wedge^{2}{\bf C}^{4}}({\bf C})
. Then, the exterior square representation defines a morphism

\wedge^{2}:\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}({\bf C})\to{\mathbf{GO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C}),\,(g,\lambda)\mapsto\lambda\cdot\wedge^{2}\!g,

as
{}^{t}(\wedge^{2}\!g)I_{6}^{\prime}(\wedge^{2}\!g)={\rm det}(g)I_{6}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
{\rm det}(\lambda\cdot\wedge^{2}\!g)=\lambda^{6}\cdot{\rm det}(g)^{3}
implies that the map
\wedge^{2}
factors through
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
. Hence, if we let
s_{\pi_{v}}
be the Frobenius conjugacy class of
\pi_{v}
(or rather
\pi^{\prime}_{v}\otimes\chi_{v}
), we regard
\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})\in{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
.

Lemma 3.9
.

We have

L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})=L(2s,\sigma_{v},\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})\sum_{m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq 0}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}|V_{(m_{1},m_{2})}\right)q_{v}^{-s(m_{1}+m_{2})},

where
V_{(m_{1},m_{2})}:=V_{(m_{1},m_{2},0,0)}\otimes W_{(m_{1},m_{2})}
is the representation of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
obtained as the tensor product of the two irreducible algebraic representations of highest weight
(m_{1},m_{2},0,0)
and
(m_{1},m_{2})
respectively.

Proof.

Since
\wedge^{2}
factors through
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
, this follows from a formula for the standard representation of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
similar to
[
19
, (A.2.1)]
. The proof is identical to the one of
loc. cit.
and we only sketch it. Let
E={\bf C}^{6\times 2}
and let
S(E^{*})
be the symmetric algebra of complex valued polynomial functions on
E
equipped with the natural
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
-action. Note that the factorization of
S(E^{*})
into irreducible
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
-representations is equivalent to the one of
\mathbf{Sym}^{\ell}(\mathrm{std}_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}})
for all
\ell\geq 0
. Let
J(E^{*})
denote the subring of
S(E^{*})
consisting of
{\mathbf{SO}}_{6}({\bf C})
-invariant polynomial functions on
E
. Equivalently,
J(E^{*})
is the algebra of polynomials on the space of matrices of the form
Y={}^{t}XI_{6}^{\prime}X
, with
X\in E
. Through this identification, a polynomial (in the variable
Y={}^{t}XI_{6}^{\prime}X
) of degree
\ell
corresponds to one of degree
2\ell
in the variable
X\in E
. Notice that
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
acts on
J(E^{*})
via the multiplier. Indeed, if
g\in{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
then
g\cdot Y={}^{t}(gX)\,I_{6}^{\prime}\,gX={}^{t}X{}^{t}g\,I_{6}^{\prime}\,gX=\nu^{\prime}(g)Y
. The action of an element
g\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
on the subspace of homogeneous polynomials (in the variable
Y
) of degree
\ell
is instead equivalent to
{\rm Sym}^{\ell}({\rm Sym}^{2}(g))
. If we let
H(E^{*})
denote the subspace of
S(E^{*})
of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
-harmonic polynomials, by
[
53
, Theorem 2.5]
we have a “separation of variables”

S(E^{*})\simeq J(E^{*})\otimes H(E^{*}),

where the isomorphism is as
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
-modules. The result then follows from decomposing
H(E^{*})
into its irreducible
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
-components, which goes as follows. Let
H(E^{*})^{(m_{1},m_{2})}
denote the subspace of
H(E^{*})
consisting of the polynomials which transform under
\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf C})
according to the representation
W_{(m_{1},m_{2})}
of highest weight
(m_{1},m_{2})
; then as a
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
-module
H(E^{*})^{(m_{1},m_{2})}
is equivalent to the irreducible representation
V_{(m_{1},m_{2},0,0)}
by
[
53
, Theorem 3.1]
. This implies that

H(E^{*})\simeq\bigoplus_{m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq 0}V_{(m_{1},m_{2},0,0)}\otimes W_{(m_{1},m_{2})}.

Hence we have that
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{Sym}^{\ell}\left(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}\right)\right)
equals to

\sum_{2i+j=\ell}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{Sym}^{i}(\nu^{\prime}(\wedge^{2}s_{\pi_{v}}){\rm Sym}^{2}(s_{\sigma_{v}})))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq 0\\
m_{1}+m_{2}=j\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})\otimes s_{\sigma_{v}}|V_{(m_{1},m_{2},0,0)}\otimes W_{(m_{1},m_{2})}).

Since the multiplier
\nu^{\prime}(\wedge^{2}s_{\pi_{v}})=\chi_{v}(\varpi_{v})^{2}{\rm det}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}})=\chi_{v}(\varpi_{v})^{2}\omega_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}(\varpi_{v})=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})
, the result follows.
∎

We can now prove the following.

Corollary 3.10
.

We have

Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v}})=L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}).

Proof.

By the isomorphism
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F_{v})
described above, we get

W_{\pi_{v}}(t(\varpi_{v}^{2n+m},\varpi_{v}^{n+m};\varpi_{v}^{2n+m}))=\chi_{v}(\varpi_{v})^{2n+m}W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}(t(\varpi^{2n+m}_{v},\varpi^{n+m}_{v},\varpi^{n}_{v},1)),

where we used the notation
t(a,b,c,d):=\mathrm{diag}(a,b,c,d)\in\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})
. By the Casselman–Shalika formula we relate the values of this Whittaker functional to traces of representations of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
as follows.
From now on we use the basis of
X^{\bullet}(T_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}})
consisting of
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}
, where when
1\leq i\leq 3
,
\alpha_{i}
is the projection
T_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}}\to\mathbf{GL}_{1}
of the
i
-th diagonal entry, and
\alpha_{4}
is the similitude character. First of all, the isomorphism
X_{\bullet}(T_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}})\simeq X^{\bullet}(T_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}})
identifies the character

\displaystyle\chi_{m,n}:T_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}\to\mathbf{GL}_{1},\;t(a,b,c,d)\mapsto a^{2n+m}b^{n+m}c^{n}

with the cocharacter

\mathbf{GL}_{1}\to T_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}},\;a\mapsto t(a^{2n+m},a^{n+m},a^{n},1).

Under the exterior square map
\wedge^{2}:(\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf C})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}({\bf C}))/\langle(zI_{4},z^{-2}),\,z\in\mathbf{GL}_{1}({\bf C})\rangle\simeq{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
, the character
\chi_{m,n}
induces the character
\tilde{\chi}_{m,n}(t):=\alpha_{1}(t)^{m+n}\alpha_{2}(t)^{n}
of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
.
Then the Casselman–Shalika formula
[
5
]
implies that

\displaystyle W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}(t(\varpi^{2n+m}_{v},\varpi^{n+m}_{v},\varpi^{n}_{v},1))=q_{v}^{-3n-2m}\,\mathrm{Tr}(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}})|V_{(m+n,n,0,0)}),

where
V_{(m+n,n,0,0)}
is the representation of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}({\bf C})
with highest weight
\tilde{\chi}_{m,n}
, and hence

(15)

\displaystyle W_{\pi_{v}}(t(\varpi_{v}^{2n+m},\varpi_{v}^{n+m};\varpi_{v}^{2n+m}))=q_{v}^{-3n-2m}\,\mathrm{Tr}(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})|V_{(m+n,n,0,0)}),

since
\mathrm{Tr}(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})|V_{(m+n,n,0,0)})=\chi_{v}(\varpi_{v})^{2n+m}\mathrm{Tr}(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}})|V_{(m+n,n,0,0)})
.
On the other hand we have

(16)

\displaystyle W_{\sigma_{v}}(\hat{t}(\varpi_{v}^{n+m},\varpi_{v}^{n}))=q_{v}^{-m/2}\,\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\sigma_{v}}|W_{(n+m,n)}).

Using (
13
), (
16
), and (
15
) we see that
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v}})
is equal to

L(2s,\sigma_{v},\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})\sum_{n,m\geq 0}q_{v}^{-s(2n+m)}\,\mathrm{Tr}(\wedge^{2}(s_{\pi_{v}})|V_{(m+n,n,0,0)})\mathrm{Tr}(s_{\sigma_{v}}|W_{(m+n,n)}).

The result then follows from Lemma
3.9
.
∎

3.5.
The ramified integrals

Recall that, for any place
v
of
F
, we have set

Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s})=\int_{U_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})Z_{\mathbf{H}}(F_{v})\setminus\mathbf{H}(F_{v})}W_{\sigma_{v},s}\left(g_{v}\right)W_{\pi_{v}}(g_{v})dg_{v},

where
W_{\sigma_{v},s}
was introduced in §
3.1.1
. Let
\Sigma_{F}
denote the set of archimedean places of
F
. We assume that the product
\omega_{\pi_{v}}\cdot\omega_{\sigma_{v}}
is unitary.

Proposition 3.11
.

There exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
so that
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s})
converges absolutely for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>1/2-\varepsilon
.

For any finite place
v
of
F
, there exist
W_{\pi_{v}}
and
W_{\sigma_{v},s}
such that
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s})
is a non-zero constant independent of
s
.

For any place
v\in\Sigma_{F}
, given
s_{0}\in{\bf C}
one can choose
W_{\pi_{v}}
and
W_{\sigma_{v},s}
such that
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\sigma_{v},s})
is non-zero at
s=s_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof of (i) is rather standard (see for instance
[
32
, §7, Proposition 1]
) and uses the asymptotic expressions for
W_{\sigma_{v},s}
and
W_{\pi_{v}}
as follows. Using the Iwasawa decomposition, it is enough to show that

(17)

\displaystyle\int_{(F_{v}^{\times})^{2}}|a|^{2s-3}|b|^{s-5/2}(\omega_{\pi_{v}}\omega_{\sigma_{v}})^{-1}(a)W_{\sigma_{v}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)W_{\pi_{v}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}ab&&&\\
&b&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&a^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)dadb,

for
W_{\sigma_{v}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}}
, converges absolutely for
{\rm Re}(s)>1/2-\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
. From the asymptotics given in
[
32
, Proposition 3, p. 177]
and
[
10
, Proposition 3.1]
, we get that

W_{\pi_{v}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}ab&&&\\
&b&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&a^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=|a|^{3}|b|^{2}\sum_{\mu\in X}\phi_{\mu}(a,b)\mu(a,b),

where
X
is a finite set of finite functions, which are linear combinations of functions on
(F_{v}^{\times})^{2}
of the form

\mu(a_{1},a_{2})=\prod_{i=1}^{2}\mu_{i}(a_{i})|a_{i}|^{u_{i}}(\log|a_{i}|)^{n_{i}},

with
\mu_{i}
unitary characters,
u_{i}\in{\bf R}
,
n_{i}\in{\bf Z}_{\geq 0}
, and the
\phi_{\mu}
’s are Schwartz functions in
\mathcal{S}(F_{v}^{2})
. Similarly,

\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{v}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}}\phi_{\mu^{\prime}}(b)\mu^{\prime}(b),

where now
X^{\prime}
is a finite set of finite functions on
F_{v}^{\times}
and the
\phi_{\mu^{\prime}}
’s are Schwartz functions in
\mathcal{S}(F_{v})
. Since
\omega_{\pi_{v}}\cdot\omega_{\sigma_{v}}
is unitary, the absolute value of
(
17
) is bounded by

\sum_{\mu\in X,\mu^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}}\int_{(F_{v}^{\times})^{2}}|a|^{2s}|b|^{s-1/2}|\phi_{\mu}(a,b)\phi_{\mu^{\prime}}(b)\mu(a,b)\mu^{\prime}(b)|dadb.

By density, we can assume that
\phi_{\mu}(a,b)
factors as a product of Schwartz functions
\phi_{\mu,1}(a)
and
\phi_{\mu,2}(b)
on
F_{v}
, so that we are left to bound
