. By matching the action of the center, we deduce that a representation
V_{\lambda}
of
{\mathbf{U}}(3)
of highest weight
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
, with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}
, decomposes into a sum of irreducible
{\mathbf{U}}(1)\times{\mathbf{U}}(2)
-representations as follows:

(45)

\displaystyle V_{\lambda}|_{{\mathbf{U}}(1)\times{\mathbf{U}}(2)}\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\bf Z}\\
\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{det}^{|\lambda|-\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\otimes V_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})},

where
|\lambda|:=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}
and
V_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}
denotes the representation of
{\mathbf{U}}(2)
of highest weight
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. By (
45
), any
{\mathbf{U}}(3)
-type of the form
(2a,2b,0)
or
(0,-2\beta,-2\gamma)
in
\Pi(\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\bf R})
contains a unique sub
{\mathbf{U}}(1)\times{\mathbf{U}}(2)
-representation, with
{\mathbf{U}}(1)
-component being the trivial representation. In contrast, no
{\mathbf{U}}(3)
-type in
\Pi(D)
has this property. Hence, if we choose
\varphi_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbf{SL}}_{2}({\bf R}))
to be right
{\mathbf{U}}(1)
-invariant, we have
\Phi_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}\in\Pi(\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\bf R})
for any
\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}({\bf R}))
. Moreover, if
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}
is
{\mathbf{U}}(1)\times{\mathbf{U}}(2)
-right invariant, the integral
z(1/2,\Phi_{\varphi},\Psi_{v})
is non-zero because, following the proof of (1), we can choose
v_{1},v_{2}\in\sigma_{v}
such that
\langle\sigma_{v}(\varphi_{1})v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\neq 0
.

When
F_{v}={\bf C}
,
[
39
, Theorem 1, Case IId]
shows that

I_{P_{(3,0)}}(\tfrac{1}{2},\mathbf{1})_{v}=\Pi(V_{\bf C}),

with
V_{\bf C}\simeq(M_{2}({\bf C}),{\rm det})
; the non-vanishing follows from (1). To conclude the proof, take
\phi_{1/2}=\otimes_{v}\phi_{1/2,v}\in\Pi(\mathbb{H}^{2})
such that
\phi_{1/2,v}
is the unramified section at any finite or complex place
v
and equals
\Phi_{\varphi}
, with
\varphi
being
{\mathbf{U}}(1)\times{\mathbf{U}}(2)
-right invariant, at any real place. Then, the section
p_{\Psi}(\phi_{1/2})
, attached to such
\phi_{1/2}
and the spherical cusp form
\Psi
, is unramified and non-zero by the discussion above.

∎

We conclude the section with a special case of Theorem
6.9
.

Corollary 6.11
.

Let
\pi
and
\sigma
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf A})
which have all local components unramified and such that
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=1.
Then,

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{J}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0,

where we recall that
L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
denotes the completed
L
-function.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.10
, we can choose
\phi_{1/2}\in\Pi(\mathbb{H}^{2})
so that
p_{\Psi}(\phi_{1/2})
is a non-trivial unramified section of
I_{P_{(1,2)}}(\tfrac{1}{2},\sigma)
. Thus, by Theorems
3.5
and
4.1
, we have

Z(1/2,\varphi,p_{\Psi}(\phi_{1/2}))=L(1/2,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}).

On the other hand, since
\pi
and
\sigma
are unramified everywhere, the Siegel Weil formula gives

Z(1/2,\varphi,p_{\Psi}(\phi_{1/2}))=2\,\zeta_{F}(2)^{2}\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}]}\int_{[\mathbf{O}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}]}\theta(hh_{1},\iota(g_{1},g_{2});f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}})\Psi^{w}(g_{1})\varphi(g_{2})\,dh\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2},

where
\phi_{1/2}=\lambda_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}})^{\sim}
. Arguing as in Theorem
6.9
, the result follows.
∎

7.
The degree
20
central
L
-value and the spherical linear period

In what follows, we restrict to the case of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
.

7.1.
Global theta liftings for general linear groups

We recall some properties of the theta lift for the type
\mathrm{II}
dual reductive pair
(\mathbf{GL}_{4},\mathbf{GL}_{4})
. We invite the reader to consult
[
56
]
for more details.

Let
\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
be the space of Schwartz function on
M_{4}({\bf A})
, where
M_{4}({\bf A})
denotes the space of
4\times 4
-matrices with entries in
{\bf A}
. The Weil representation
\omega_{4,4}
of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
on
\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
is defined by

(\omega_{4,4}(h,g)f)(x)=|{\rm det}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-2}|{\rm det}(g)|_{\bf A}^{2}f(h^{-1}xg),

for every
g,h\in\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
.

Denote by
\mathcal{S}_{0}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
the space of
K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}\times K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}
-finite vectors of
\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
, where
K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}
is the standard maximal compact subgroup of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
.
Let
\pi
be a cuspidal representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
. For a cusp form
\varphi\in\pi
, a function
f\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
, and a complex number
z\in{\bf C}
, define

\displaystyle\varphi^{z}_{f}(h)

\displaystyle:=\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}\varphi(g)|\mathrm{det}(g)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}(\omega_{4,4}(h,g)f)(x)dg

(46)

\displaystyle\,=|\mathrm{det}(h)|^{-2}_{{\bf A}}\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}\varphi(g)|\mathrm{det}(g)|^{z+2}_{{\bf A}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}f(h^{-1}xg)dg.

This integral is absolutely convergent for
\mathrm{Re}(z)
big enough and can be analytically continued to all
{\bf C}
as an entire function, which we still call
\varphi^{z}_{f}
, by
[
56
, Lemma 3]
. Moreover,
\varphi^{z}_{f}
is a (possibly zero) automorphic form on
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and is a cusp form for
\mathrm{Re}(z)
big enough (see
[
56
, p. 707]
).
For a fixed
z\in{\bf C}
, let
\Theta^{z}(\pi)
be the space spanned by
\varphi^{z}_{f}(h)
as
\varphi
varies in the space of
\pi
and
f
among the subspace of
\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
of
K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}\times K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}
-finite vectors. If
\omega_{\pi}
denotes the central character of
\pi
, then
\Theta^{z}(\pi)
has central character
\omega_{\pi}(\cdot)|\cdot|^{4z}_{\bf A}
.

Proposition 7.1

(
[
56
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
L(z,\pi,{\rm std}_{4})
be the standard
L
-function of
\pi
. Then, the space
\Theta^{0}(\pi)
is non-zero if and only if
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})\neq 0
. In this case,
\Theta^{0}(\pi)
coincides with
\pi
.

The Proposition implies that, if
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})\neq 0
, for any cusp form
\varphi^{\prime}\in\pi
, there exist a cusp form
\varphi\in\pi
and a
K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}\times K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}
-finite function
f\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
such that
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi^{0}_{f}
. Thanks to the following Lemma, one can choose such
f\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
with support away from
0
.

Lemma 7.2
.

For any cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
with
\Theta^{0}(\pi)=\pi
and any
\varphi^{\prime}\in\pi
, there exists
\varphi\in\pi
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
with
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}(f)
such that
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi^{0}_{f}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}:=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})//K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}})
denote the Hecke algebra of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
. The Weil representation
\omega_{4,4}
defines a representation of
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}\times\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}
on the space
\mathcal{S}_{0}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
. By
[
56
, Proof of Theorem 2]
, the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}(M_{4}({\bf A}))\otimes\pi\otimes\pi^{\vee}

\displaystyle\to{\bf C},

\displaystyle(f,\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\varphi_{1,f}^{0},\varphi_{2}\rangle,

with
\langle\,,\,\rangle
denoting the Petersson inner product, is
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}\times\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}
-equivariant. By duality, we thus get a
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}\times\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}
-equivariant map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}(M_{4}({\bf A}))\otimes\pi\to\pi,\,\,\,(f,\varphi)\mapsto\varphi_{f}^{0},

such that
(\beta*f*\alpha,\alpha*\varphi)\mapsto(\alpha*\varphi)^{0}_{\beta*f*\alpha}=\beta*(\alpha*\varphi)^{0}_{f*\alpha}=\beta*\varphi^{0}_{f}
for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}
.
Suppose now that
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi^{0}_{f^{\prime}}
for some
\varphi\in\pi
and
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
. For any non-trivial
\alpha\in\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}}
such that
\int_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})}\alpha(r)dr=0
, we have that
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}(f^{\prime}*\alpha)
. By Hecke equivariance, it thus follows that
\varphi^{\prime}=(\alpha*\varphi)^{0}_{f^{\prime}*\alpha}
, as desired.
∎

7.2.
The two variable zeta integral

In this section, we use the theta correspondence for
(\mathbf{GL}_{4},\mathbf{GL}_{4})
to upgrade the zeta integral of §
3
to a two complex variable integral.

Fix
\pi\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{4}])
and
\sigma\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{2}])
. Let
s,z\in{\bf C}
. Given a cusp form
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}\in\pi
, a section
f_{s}=\otimes_{v}f_{s,v}\in I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})
, and a factorizable Schwartz function
\eta
on
M_{4}({\bf A})
, define

J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta):=\int_{{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F)Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})\setminus{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!\!\!E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})\varphi_{\eta}^{z}(g)|{\rm det}(g)|_{\bf A}^{-z}dg.

For
{\rm Re}(z)
large enough, this is well defined as
\varphi_{\eta}^{z}
is a cusp form of central character
\omega_{\pi}(\cdot)|\cdot|^{4z}_{\bf A}
. Fix a finite set of places
S
outside of which the data
(\varphi,f_{s},\eta)
is unramified. Then we have the following.

Lemma 7.3
.

For big enough
{\rm Re}(s)
and
{\rm Re}(z)
, we have

J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)=L^{S}(2s,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi})\int_{U_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})\setminus{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}({\bf A})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!W_{\sigma,s}\left(g\right)W_{\varphi,\eta}^{z}(g)|{\rm det}(g)|_{\bf A}^{-z}dg,

where
W_{\sigma,s}
is as in (
6
), while
W_{\varphi,\eta}^{z}
denotes the Whittaker model of
\varphi_{\eta}^{z}
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to the one of Proposition
3.2
.
∎

By the proof of
[
56
, Theorem 1]
, the Whittaker model of
\varphi_{\eta}^{z}
can be expressed as

W_{\varphi,\eta}^{z}(g)=|{\rm det}(g)|_{\bf A}^{z}\int_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})}W_{\varphi}(gh)|{\rm det}(h)|_{\bf A}^{z+2}\eta(h)dh.

Write
\varphi=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\varphi_{v}
and
\eta=\prod_{v}\eta_{v}
, then

W_{\varphi,\eta}^{z}(g)=\prod_{v}W_{\varphi_{v},\eta_{v}}^{z}(g_{v}),

where

(47)

\displaystyle W_{\varphi_{v},\eta_{v}}^{z}(g_{v})=|{\rm det}(g_{v})|_{v}^{z}\int_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})}W_{\varphi_{v}}(g_{v}h_{v})|{\rm det}(h_{v})|_{v}^{z+2}\eta_{v}(h_{v})dh_{v}.

Recall also that
W_{\sigma,s}(g)
decomposes as
\prod_{v}W_{\sigma_{v},s}(g_{v})
, with
W_{\sigma_{v},s}(g_{v})
equal to the spherical vector
W_{s,v}^{\rm o}(g_{v})
of (
10
) at almost all
v
.

Theorem 7.4
.

For big enough
{\rm Re}(s)
and
{\rm Re}(z)
, we have the equality

J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)=L^{S}(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\prod_{v\in S}J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v}),

where

J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v}):=\int_{U_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F_{v})Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F_{v})\setminus{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F_{v})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!W_{\sigma_{v},s}\left(g_{v}\right)W_{\varphi_{v},\eta_{v}}^{z}(g_{v})|{\rm det}(g_{v})|_{v}^{-z}dg_{v}.

Proof.

Lemma
7.3
implies that, for big enough
{\rm Re}(s)
and
{\rm Re}(z)
,

\frac{J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)}{L^{S}(2s,\sigma,{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi})}=\prod_{v}J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v}).

Let now
v\not\in S
. Then
W_{\varphi_{v}}
is the spherical Whittaker function and
\eta_{v}={\rm char}(M_{4}(\mathcal{O}_{v}))
. By
[
21
, Lemma 6.10]
, together with
[
56
, (1.5) & (2.1)]
, we have

(48)

\displaystyle W_{\varphi_{v},\eta_{v}}^{z}(g_{v})=L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{v},{\rm std}_{4})|{\rm det}(g_{v})|_{v}^{z}W_{\varphi_{v}}(g_{v}).

We plug (
48
) into
J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v})
to write

\displaystyle J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v})

\displaystyle=L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{v},{\rm std}_{4})\int_{U_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F_{v})Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F_{v})\setminus{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}}(F_{v})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!W_{s,v}^{\rm o}\left(g_{v}\right)W_{\varphi_{v}}(g_{v})dg_{v}.

The integral of the right hand side equals
L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})^{-1}\cdot Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{s,v}^{\rm o})
, where
Z(s,W_{\pi_{v}},W_{s,v}^{\rm o})
is the integral calculated in §
3.4
. Hence, Corollaries
3.8
and
3.10
give

\displaystyle J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v})

\displaystyle=\frac{L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{v},{\rm std}_{4})L(s,\pi_{v}\otimes\sigma_{v},\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})}{L(2s,\sigma_{v},{\rm Sym}^{2}\otimes\omega_{\pi_{v}})},

thus

J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)=L^{S}(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\prod_{v\in S}J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v}),

as desired.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose that
v\in S
, then, as a function of
z
,

J^{\star}(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v}):=L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{v},{\rm std}_{4})^{-1}J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v})

has holomorphic continuation to all
{\bf C}
and there exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that it converges absolutely for
{\rm Re}(s)>1/2-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

When
v\in S
, let
\Omega
be an open compact subgroup of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})
such that
\eta_{v}(k_{v}g_{v})=\eta_{v}(g_{v})
for all
k_{v}\in\Omega
and fix a basis
\{W_{v,1},\dots,W_{v,m}\}
of the
\Omega
-invariant vectors in the Whittaker model of
\pi_{v}
. The results
[
56
, (1.5) & (2.1)]
give (see also p. 708 of
loc.cit.
)

W_{\varphi_{v},\eta_{v}}^{z}(g_{v})=L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{v},{\rm std}_{4})|{\rm det}(g_{v})|_{v}^{z}\sum_{j}\Xi_{v,j}(z)W_{v,j}(g_{v}),

where
\Xi_{v,j}(z)
are entire functions on
z
. Hence,

J(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v})=L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{v},{\rm std}_{4})\sum_{j}\Xi_{v,j}(z)Z(s,W_{v,j},W_{\sigma_{v}}),

where
Z(s,W_{v,j},W_{\sigma_{v},s})
denotes the integral of §
3.5
. The result follows from this equality, together with Proposition
3.11
(i).
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose that
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=1
. Given a cusp form
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}\in\pi
, a section
f=f_{v}\in I_{P_{(1,2)}}(1/2,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})
, and a Schwartz function
\eta=\otimes_{v}\eta_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
, we have

J(1/2,0,\varphi,f,\eta)=L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L^{S}(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\prod_{v\in S}J^{\star}(1/2,0,\varphi_{v},f_{v},\eta_{v}).

Proof.

Choose a standard holomorphic section
f_{s}\in I_{P_{(1,2)}}(s,\sigma,\omega_{\pi})
such that
f_{1/2}=f
. Thanks to Theorem
7.4
, we have that, for large enough
{\rm Re}(s)
and
{\rm Re}(z)
,

(49)

\displaystyle J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)=L(z+\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\prod_{v\in S}J^{\star}(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v}).

The Klingen Eisenstein series
E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})
is entire when
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=1
(see §
3.1
), thus
J(s,z,\varphi,f_{s},\eta)
has holomorphic continuation in the variable
s
to all
{\bf C}
. Moreover, by Lemma
7.5
and the holomorphicity of the standard
L
-function of
\pi
, we deduce that both sides have holomorphic continuation in the variable
z
. By Lemma
7.5
, we also know that the ramified zeta factor
J^{\star}(s,z,\varphi_{v},f_{s,v},\eta_{v})
converges absolutely whenever
{\rm Re}(s)>1/2-\varepsilon
, for some
\varepsilon>0
. Thus, we obtain the desired formula by evaluating (
49
) at
s=1/2
and
z=0
.
∎

7.3.
The interplay between two seesaw identities

Let
\pi
, resp.
\sigma
, be cuspidal automorphic representations of

\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
, resp.
\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf A})
, such that

\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=1
.
As in §
6.2
, we use Garrett’s pullback formula (Corollary
5.3
) and the Siegel-Weil formula of Theorem
6.2
to deduce that, for
{\rm Re}(z)>>0
, we have

\displaystyle J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}]}E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(\iota(g_{1},g_{2}),\tfrac{1}{2},\phi_{\frac{1}{2}})\Psi^{w}(g_{1})\varphi^{z}_{\eta}(g_{2})|\nu(g_{2})|_{\bf A}^{-2z}\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2}

\displaystyle=2\,\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}\sum_{V}\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}]}\int_{[\mathbf{O}_{V}]}\!\!\!\!\!\theta(hh_{1},\iota(g_{1},g_{2});f^{V})\Psi^{w}(g_{1})\varphi^{z}_{\eta}(g_{2})|\nu(g_{2})|_{\bf A}^{-2z}\,dh\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2}

(50)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}\sum_{V}\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}]}\int_{[{\mathbf{SO}}_{V}]}\!\!\!\!\!\theta(hh_{1},\iota(g_{1},g_{2});f^{V})\Psi^{w}(g_{1})\varphi^{z}_{\eta}(g_{2})|\nu(g_{2})|_{\bf A}^{-2z}\,dh\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2},

where the latter equality follows from Lemma
6.3
. Recall also that
V
ranges through the (finite) set of quaternionic quadratic spaces of dimension
4
over
F
for which the image of
\phi_{1/2}
via the projection
{\rm pr}_{\Pi(V)}:I_{P_{(3,0)}}(1/2,\mathbf{1})\to\Pi(V)
is non-zero, and
f^{V}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
is such that
{\rm pr}_{\Pi(V)}\phi_{1/2}=\lambda_{V}(f^{V})^{\sim}
.

Now consider the factor of the sum (
50
) corresponding to
f=f^{V}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
. Recall the seesaw pair

\textstyle{\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\boxtimes\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{GSO}_{V}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4},}

where the vertical left line denotes the diagonal embedding
\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\boxtimes\mathbf{GSO}_{V}
.
To obtain the corresponding seesaw identity, we first record the following Lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

For
f=f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})\subset\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
, we have the following equality:

(51)

\displaystyle\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{V}]}\theta(hh_{1},\iota(g_{1},g_{2});f)dh=\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{V}]}\theta(hh_{1},g_{1};f_{1})\theta(hh_{1},g_{2};f_{2})dh,

where each theta function is regularized as in
[
27
]
when
V
is split.

Proof.

The result is straightforward when
V
is non-split; when
V
is split, to obtain the desired equality one has to check that each side of (
51
) can be compatibly regularized. This follows from the results of
[
27
]
, as we now explain.

Let
V
be split and let
\omega(z_{2})
,
\omega(z_{4})
and
\omega(z_{6})
be the regularization operators defined in
[
27
, Lemma 1.3]
for the dual reductive pairs
(\mathbf{O}_{V},{\mathbf{Sp}}_{2})
,
(\mathbf{O}_{V},{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4})
and
(\mathbf{O}_{V},{\mathbf{Sp}}_{6})
, respectively. We then let
c_{2},c_{4},c_{6}
be the constants of
[
27
, Lemma 1.6]
attached to
z_{2}
,
z_{4}
and
z_{6}
, respectively. We define the following two functionals:

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})\to{\bf C},\,\,f\otimes f^{\prime}\mapsto(c_{2}c_{4})^{-1}\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{V}]}\theta(h,I_{2},\omega(z_{2})f)\theta(h,I_{4},\omega(z_{4})f^{\prime})dh,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})\to{\bf C},\,\,f^{\prime\prime}\mapsto c_{6}^{-1}\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{V}]}\theta(h,I_{6},\omega(z_{6})f^{\prime\prime})dh.

By the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 1.5]
, the theta functions

\theta(h,I_{2},\omega(z_{2})f),\;\theta(h,I_{4},\omega(z_{4})f^{\prime}),\;\theta(h,I_{6},\omega(z_{6})f^{\prime\prime})

are rapidly decreasing. Thus, the functionals
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are well defined for any Schwartz function
f,f^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime\prime}
. The result follows from
[
27
, Lemma 1.9]
, once we show that the functional
I_{1}
extends to a functional
\tilde{I}_{1}
on
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
and that
\tilde{I}_{1}
equals
I_{2}
.
Firstly, using the fact that
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})
is dense in
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
, the functional
I_{1}
extends to an
\mathbf{SO}_{V}({\bf A})
-invariant functional
\tilde{I}_{1}:\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})\to{\bf C}
. Moreover, using again the density of
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})
in
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
,

\tilde{I}_{1}(f)=I_{2}(f)=\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{V}]}\theta(h,I_{6},f)dh,

for any Schwartz function
f\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
such that the theta integral is absolutely convergent. This and
[
27
, Lemma 1.9]
(which applies to these
\mathbf{SO}_{V}({\bf A})
-invariant functionals because of Lemma
6.3
) conclude the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Suppose that
f=f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})\subset\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{3})
. By Lemma
7.7
and following verbatim the argument of
[
23
, Lemma 5.1]
, we get the seesaw identity

\displaystyle\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}]}\int_{[{\mathbf{SO}}_{V}]}\!\!\!\!\!\theta(hh_{1},\iota(g_{1},g_{2});f)\Psi^{w}(g_{1})\varphi^{z}_{\eta}(g_{2})|\nu(g_{2})|_{\bf A}^{-2z}\,dh\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V}}({\bf A})\backslash[{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V}]}\int_{[{\mathbf{SL}}_{2}\times{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\!\!\!\!\!\theta(h,g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1};f_{1})\theta(h,g_{2}g^{\prime}_{2};f_{2})\Psi^{w}(g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1})\varphi^{z}_{\eta}(g_{2}g^{\prime}_{2})|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-2z}\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2}\,dh

\displaystyle=\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V}}({\bf A})\backslash[{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V}]}\int_{[{\mathbf{SL}}_{2}\times{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\!\!\!\!\!\theta(h,g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1};f_{1})\theta(h,g_{2}g^{\prime}_{2};f_{2})\Psi^{w}(g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1})|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-2z}\cdot

(52)

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}\varphi(r)|\mathrm{det}(r)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}\omega_{4,4}(g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},r)\eta(x)\,dr\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2}\,dh,

where
g^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}
and
g_{2}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}
are chosen so that
\nu(g_{1}^{\prime})=\nu(g_{2}^{\prime})=\nu^{\prime}(h)
and where the last equality follows from (
46
).

The following Proposition and Lemma concern a second seesaw pair, which involves the theta correspondence for
(\mathbf{GL}_{4},\mathbf{GL}_{4})
and the symplectic-orthogonal theta correspondences. Namely, if we let
V^{\prime}:=V\oplus\mathbb{H}^{4}
, the discussion below can be summarized by saying that there is a seesaw pair

\textstyle{\mathbf{GSO}_{V^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4},}

where the right vertical line corresponds to the diagonal embedding
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}
, while the left vertical line is given by

\displaystyle j:\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{GSO}_{V^{\prime}},

\displaystyle(h,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}r&&\\
&h&\\
&&\nu^{\prime}(h)I_{4}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}r^{-1}I_{4}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
be a Schwartz function with support outside
0
. There is a
\mathbf{GSO}_{V}({\bf A})\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
-equivariant isomorphism
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})\otimes\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))\to\mathcal{S}(V^{\prime}({\bf A})^{2})
, such that, for any
g_{2}\in{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}({\bf A})
,

\theta(h,g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime};f_{2})\cdot\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}\omega_{4,4}(g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},r)\eta(x)=\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta)),

where the theta function appearing on the right hand side is the theta function with respect to the dual reductive pair
(\mathbf{O}_{V^{\prime}},{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4})
and polarization
\left(V^{\prime}\otimes W_{4}^{+}\right)\oplus\left(V^{\prime}\otimes W_{4}^{-}\right)
.

Proof.

The factorization

(53)

\left(V\otimes W_{4}^{\vee,-}\oplus(\mathbb{H}^{\vee,+})^{4}\otimes W_{4}^{\vee}\right)\oplus\left(V\otimes W_{4}^{\vee,+}\oplus(\mathbb{H}^{\vee,-})^{4}\otimes W_{4}^{\vee}\right),

defines a polarization of
(V\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\vee,4})\otimes W_{4}^{\vee}
. By
[
41
, Chapitre 2, I.7]
, there exists an intertwining operator

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})\otimes\mathcal{S}(W_{4}^{\vee}({\bf A})^{4})\to\mathcal{S}(V^{\prime}({\bf A})^{2}),

given by the partial Fourier transform that relates the polarization (
53
) with the polarization

\left(V^{\prime}\otimes W_{4}^{+}\right)\oplus\left(V^{\prime}\otimes W_{4}^{-}\right).

By Poisson summation formula, we then have that, for
f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})
and
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(W_{4}^{\vee}({\bf A})^{4})
,

(54)

\theta(j(h,r),g_{2},\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))=\sum_{y\in V\otimes W_{4}^{\vee,-}}\sum_{x\in(\mathbb{H}^{\vee,+})^{4}\otimes W_{4}^{\vee}}\omega(j(h,r),g_{2})(f_{2}\otimes\eta)(y,x),

for any
g_{2}\in{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}({\bf A})
and
(h,r)\in{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V}({\bf A})\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
such that
\nu(g_{2})=\nu^{\prime}(h)
. We now compare
\theta(j(h,r),g_{2},\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))
with

(55)

\displaystyle\theta(h,g_{2};f_{2})\cdot\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}\omega_{4,4}(g_{2},r)\eta(x)

\displaystyle=|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-4}|{\rm det}(r)|_{\bf A}^{2}\sum_{y\in V\otimes W_{4}^{+}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}\omega(h,g_{2})f_{2}(y)\eta(g_{2}^{-1}xr).

We fix an isomorphism
6
6

6

Our choice is in line with the one of Kudla for a similar seesaw dual pair described in
[
37
, (2.22) & (2.23)]
.

M_{4}(F)\simeq(\mathbb{H}^{\vee,+})^{4}\otimes W_{4}^{\vee},

such that if
M\mapsto w\otimes v
, with
w\in(\mathbb{H}^{\vee,+})^{4}
and
v\in W_{4}^{\vee}
, then

g^{-1}Mr\mapsto(r,g)\cdot(w\,\otimes\,v):=(I_{4}^{\prime}{}^{t}r^{-1}I_{4}^{\prime})^{-1}w\,\otimes\,vg.

Via this identification, we can write (
55
) as

|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-4}|{\rm det}(r)|_{\bf A}^{2}\sum_{y\in V\otimes W_{4}^{+}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\mathbb{H}^{+})^{4}\otimes W_{4}^{\vee}\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}\omega(h,g_{2})f_{2}(y)\eta\left((r,g_{2})\cdot x\right).

The symplectic pairing gives a
{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}(F)
-equivariant isomorphism
W_{4}\simeq W_{4}^{\vee}
which induces
W_{4}^{+}\simeq W_{4}^{\vee,-}
. Using this, if we let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
be a Schwartz function with support outside
0
, the sums in
(
54
) and (
55
) agree up to verifying that the actions of
h
,
r
, and
g_{2}
coincide. Recall that the action of
\omega(j(h,r),g_{2})
in
(
54
) depends on the polarization (
53
). Since the group
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
embeds via
j
into the Levi component of the maximal parabolic of
\mathbf{GSO}_{V^{\prime}}
that stabilizes
(\mathbb{H}^{+})^{4}
, we deduce that it acts trivially on
V
and

\omega(j(1,r),1)\eta(x)=|\mathrm{det}(r)|_{{\bf A}}^{2}\eta((r,1)\cdot x).

Moreover,
h\in\mathbf{GSO}_{V}
acts by left translation by
h^{-1}
on
f_{2}
and by multiplication by
|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{{\bf A}}^{-6}
(because of (
4
)). Similarly, for the theta function
\theta(h,g_{2};f_{2})
for the pair
(\mathbf{GSO}_{V},{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4})
,
h\in\mathbf{GSO}_{V}({\bf A})
acts by left translation by
h^{-1}
together with multiplication by
|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{{\bf A}}^{-2}
. These two actions coincide up to
|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{{\bf A}}^{-4}
, which, indeed, appears as a multiplying factor in (
55
). The comparison for the action of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}({\bf A})
follows in a similar way.
∎

Lemma 7.9
.

When
V\simeq\mathbb{H}^{2}
, for any
f_{2}\otimes\eta\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})\otimes\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
, the function
\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))
is absolutely convergent in the variable
g_{2}
. Furthermore,

\int_{[{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(\omega(z_{4})f_{2}\otimes\eta))dg_{2}=\int_{[{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\tilde{\omega}(z_{4})\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))dg_{2},

where
\tilde{\omega}(z_{4})
is the regularized operator for the dual reductive pair
(\mathbf{O}_{V^{\prime}},{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4})
defined in
[
27
]
.

Proof.

We begin by showing that
\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(\omega(z_{4})f_{2}\otimes\eta))
is rapidly decreasing in
g_{2}\in{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}({\bf A})
.
Firstly, we prove that
\theta(h,g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime};\omega(z_{4})f_{2})
is rapidly decreasing in
g_{2}
, by mimicking the strategy used in
[
27
, Proposition 1.5]
and
[
36
, Proposition 5.3.1]
. By the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 1.5]
,

(56)

\omega(z_{4})\hat{f_{2}}(w)=0,\textit{ for all }w\in W_{4}({\bf A})^{2}\textit{ with }\mathrm{rank}(w)<2,

where
\hat{f_{2}}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{4}({\bf A})^{2})
is the partial Fourier transform of
f_{2}
constructed in
[
36
, (5.2.4)]
; as shown in
[
27
, Proposition 1.5]
, this fact implies that the theta function
\theta(h,g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime};\omega(z_{4})f_{2})
is rapidly decreasing in
h\in\mathbf{O}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})
.
Then, identifying

V({\bf A})^{2}\simeq W_{4}({\bf A})^{2}
as vector spaces, (
56
) implies that

\omega(z_{4})f_{2}(w^{\prime})=0,\textit{ for all }w^{\prime}\in V({\bf A})^{2}\textit{ with }\mathrm{rank}(w^{\prime})<2,

thus, following again the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 1.5]
, one has that
\theta(h,g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime};\omega(z_{4})f_{2})
is rapidly decreasing in
g_{2}
, proving our claim. Since the theta function

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}\omega_{4,4}(g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},r)\eta(x)

has moderate growth in
g_{2}
, Proposition
7.8
and the rapid decay of
\theta(h,g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime};\omega(z_{4})f_{2})
in
g_{2}
imply that

\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(\omega(z_{4})f_{2}\otimes\eta))

is rapidly decreasing in
g_{2}
.
The rapid decay of these functions implies that the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(V({\bf A})^{2})\otimes\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))

\displaystyle\to{\bf C},

\displaystyle f\otimes f^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto\int_{[{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\theta(I_{12},g_{2},\mathcal{F}(\omega(z_{4})f\otimes f^{\prime}))dg_{2},

is well-defined. The desired equality is then proved as in Lemma
7.7
.
∎

Proposition
7.8
and Lemma
7.9
show that (
52
) equals

\displaystyle\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V^{\prime}}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GSO}_{V}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}

\displaystyle|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-2z}|\mathrm{det}(r)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\varphi(r)\cdot

\displaystyle\left(\int_{[{\mathbf{SL}}_{2}]}\theta(h,g_{1}g_{1}^{\prime},f_{1})\Psi^{w}(g_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})dg_{1}\right)\left(\int_{[{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))dg_{2}\right)dh\,dr,

where we have omitted the regularization operators to simplify notation.

Remark 7.10
.

The inner integral over
[{\mathbf{SL}}_{2}]
is exactly the one studied by Shimizu
[
50
]
and Waldspurger
[
54
]
. In particular, when
V
is given by a non-split quaternion algebra
D
over
F
, if
\sigma
does not lie in the image of the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence from
D^{\times}
, then the integral above is zero.

From now on, we restrict our attention to the case of the totally split quadratic space
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and assume the following:

(
Spl
)

\phi_{1/2}\in\Pi(\mathbb{H}^{2})
,
i.e.
there exists
f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A})^{3})
such that
\lambda_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}})^{\sim}=\phi_{1/2}.

Proposition 7.11
.

Assume (
(Spl)
) and let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
be a Schwartz function with support outside
0
. If
\lambda_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})^{\sim}=\phi_{1/2},
for
f_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A}))
and
f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A})^{2})
, we have, for
{\rm Re}(z)>>0
, the equality

\displaystyle J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-2z}|\mathrm{det}(r)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\varphi(r)\cdot

\displaystyle\left(\int_{[{\mathbf{SL}}_{2}]}\theta(h,g_{1}g_{1}^{\prime},f_{1})\Psi^{w}(g_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})dg_{1}\right)\left(\int_{[{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\theta(j(h,r),g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))dg_{2}\right)dh\,dr.

Proof.

Thanks to (
(Spl)
), (
50
) reads as

\displaystyle J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)=\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}]}\int_{[{\mathbf{SO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}]}\!\!\!\!\!\theta(hh_{1},\iota(g_{1},g_{2});f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}})\Psi^{w}(g_{1})\varphi^{z}_{\eta}(g_{2})|\nu(g_{2})|_{\bf A}^{-2z}\,dh\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2},

with
f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}=f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
. The result then follows from applying the two seesaw identities above.
∎

Remark 7.12
.

(1)

If
\sigma
and
\pi
are unramified everywhere, then (
(Spl)
) holds.

(2)

If
\sigma
does not lie in the image of the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence from any non-split quaternion algebra, then one can show that
J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)
equals
J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}^{\prime}},\eta)
, with
\phi_{1/2}^{\prime}
satisfying (
(Spl)
).

Remark 7.13
.

We impose the hypothesis (
(Spl)
) for the following reason. In order to continue with the computation we relate the integral of the theta function
\theta(-,-,\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))
over
{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}
to an orthogonal Eisenstein series on
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}
via a Siegel–Weil type formula and proceed as in §
6
.
When
V\not\simeq\mathbb{H}^{2}
, this Siegel–Weil type formula does not seem to be available in the literature. Despite this, such a formula should not be essential. Instead, one should be able to replace its use by an explicit relation between the integral over
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
of the cusp form
\varphi
with the theta function
\theta(-,-,\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))
for the pair
({\mathbf{GSp}}_{4},{\mathbf{GSO}}_{V^{\prime}})
and the Jacquet–Langlands lift of
\varphi
to the inner form of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
associated with
V
. We hope to return to this problem in future work.

7.4.
A Siegel-Weil formula for
({\mathbf{GSp}}_{4},{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}})
in the first term range

We recall a Siegel-Weil formula proved by Yamana in
[
57
, Theorem 2.2 (i)]
.

Recall that
Q_{(6,0)}=M_{(6,0)}N_{(6,0)}
denotes the Siegel parabolic subgroup of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}
, with
M_{(6,0)}\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{6}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}
. For any
q=mn\in Q_{(6,0)}
, we have

\delta_{Q_{(6,0)}}(q)=\frac{|\mathrm{det}(A)|^{5}}{|\nu^{\prime}|^{15}},\text{ where $m=(A,\nu^{\prime})\in\mathbf{GL}_{6}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}$.}

We consider the normalized induced representation
I_{Q_{(6,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1}):=\mathrm{Ind}_{Q_{(6,0)}({\bf A})}^{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}({\bf A})}(\delta_{Q_{(6,0)}}^{\frac{s}{5}})
. Given a standard holomorphic section
\Phi_{s}\in I_{Q_{(6,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1})
, define the Eisenstein series

E_{Q_{(6,0)}}(\tilde{h},\Phi_{s}):=\sum_{\gamma\in Q_{(6,0)}(F)\setminus{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}(F)}\Phi_{s}(\gamma\tilde{h}).

It converges absolutely for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>>0
and admits a meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane. According to
[
57
, Theorem 2.1]
, it is holomorphic at
s=-\tfrac{1}{2}
. For the following result, denote by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{6}({\bf A})^{2})\to\mathcal{S}(W_{4}({\bf A})^{6})
,
f\mapsto\hat{f}
, the partial Fourier transform associated with the change of polarization.

Theorem 7.14
.

For every
\tilde{h}\in{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}({\bf A})
and
g_{2}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}({\bf A})
such that
\nu^{\prime}(\tilde{h})=\nu(g_{2}^{\prime})
, we have

\int_{[{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}]}\theta(\tilde{h},g_{2}g_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta))dg_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot E_{Q_{(6,0)}}\left(\tilde{h},\Phi_{-\frac{1}{2}}\right),

where, if
\tilde{h}=mnk\in L_{(6,0)}({\bf A})N_{(6,0)}({\bf A})K_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}({\bf A})}
with
m=(A,\nu^{\prime})
, we let

\Phi_{s}(\tilde{h}):=\omega(\tilde{h},g_{2}^{\prime})\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(f_{2}\otimes\eta)(0)\cdot\left|\tfrac{\mathrm{det}(A)}{\nu^{\prime}}\right|^{1/2+s},

with
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(f_{2}\otimes\eta)
denoting the Fourier transform of
\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\otimes\eta)
.

Proof.

This is
[
57
, Theorem 2.2 (i)]
in the case
3
with
m=4
,
r=2
, and
n=6
in the notation of
loc.cit.
.
∎

7.5.
The Shimizu–Jacquet–Langlands correspondence

Let
D
be a quaternion algebra defined over
F
, which, equipped with the reduced norm
\rm{Nm}_{D}
, is a quadratic space of dimension
4
. Recall from §
2.3.2
that we have an exceptional isomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathbf{GSO}}_{D}

\displaystyle\to D^{\times}\times D^{\times}/\{(z,z),\;z\in Z_{D^{\times}}\},

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto(h_{1},h_{2}),

This identifies any automorphic representation of
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{D}
with
\Pi_{1}\boxtimes\Pi_{2}
, where
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
are automorphic representations of
D^{\times}
satisfying
\omega_{\Pi_{1}}=\omega_{\Pi_{2}}^{-1}
. Accordingly, if
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are forms in the space of
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
, we write

(\Psi_{1}\boxtimes\Psi_{2})(h):=\Psi_{1}(h_{1})\Psi_{2}(h_{2}),

where
h
corresponds to
(h_{1},h_{2})
under the above isomorphism.

Theorem 7.15

(
[
54
, Théorème 1]
,
[
50
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\sigma
be any cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
. For any cusp form
\Psi\in\sigma
and any
f\in\mathcal{S}(D({\bf A}))
, we have

\int_{[{\mathbf{SL}}_{2}]}\theta(h,g_{1}g_{1}^{\prime},f)\Psi(g_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})dg_{1}=(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(h),

where
\Psi^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime\prime}
are forms in
\sigma^{D}
, with
\sigma^{D}
denoting the inverse of the Jacquet–Langlands lift of
\sigma
. If
\sigma
is not in the image of the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence, the above integral is zero.

Remark 7.16
.

Recall that, if
D\simeq\mathbb{H}^{2}
, we have
\sigma^{D}=\sigma
.

For the next Proposition, we consider the quotient
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}
by
\mathbf{GL}_{1}
via the embedding

(57)

\displaystyle\mathbf{GL}_{1}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1},

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto(aI_{4},a^{2}).

Proposition 7.17
.

Assume (
(Spl)
) with
\lambda_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})^{\sim}=\phi_{1/2},
for
f_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A}))
and
f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A})^{2})
. Let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
have support outside
0
. Then, for
{\rm Re}(z)>>0
, we have that
J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)
is equal to

\frac{\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}}{4}\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{1}\backslash(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1})]}\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}]}|a|_{\bf A}^{-2z}|\mathrm{det}(r)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\varphi(r)(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{a})\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(6,0)}}(j(ht_{a},r),\Phi_{-\frac{1}{2}})dhdrda,

where
t_{a}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{2}&\\
&aI_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and
\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}
corresponds, via Theorem
7.15
, to the pair
(\Psi^{w},f_{1})\in\sigma\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A}))
.

Proof.

Using Theorems
7.15
and
7.14
, we have that
J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)
is equal to

\frac{\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}}{2}\int_{Z_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{\bf A}^{-2z}|\mathrm{det}(r)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\varphi(r)(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(h)\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(6,0)}}(j(h,r),\Phi_{-\frac{1}{2}})dhdr.

Since
{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\simeq\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\rtimes\mathbf{GL}_{1}
and since
\mathrm{vol}([Z_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}}])=2
, we perform an outer integration over
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}
to reduce the integral over
Z_{{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}]
to the one of the statement.
∎

7.6.
A pullback formula for orthogonal groups

In order to compute the integral in Proposition
7.17
, we perform a series of computations similar to the ones that appear in §
6
. As a first step, we prove a pullback formula of Eisenstein series for orthogonal groups.

Consider the embedding

\displaystyle\kappa:{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}

\displaystyle\to{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}},

\displaystyle h\times x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}&&x_{2}\\
&h&\\
x_{3}&&x_{4}\end{smallmatrix}\right),

where
x=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
x_{3}&x_{4}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. We now study the integral

(58)

\displaystyle\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}]}

\displaystyle(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)})\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(6,0)}}(\kappa(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)},x),\Phi_{s})dh,

and relate it to an Eisenstein series on
\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}
.

Proposition 7.18
.

We have

Q^{\circ}_{(6,0)}(F)\setminus\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}(F)/\kappa(\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}})(F)=\{q_{i}\}_{i=1,2,3}.

Furthermore, the stabilizers are

(1)

\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}}(q_{1})=Q^{\circ}_{(2,0)}(F)\times Q^{\circ}_{(4,0)}(F)
.

(2)

\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}}(q_{2})
equals

\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m&&\\
&h&\\
&&m^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\times\left(\begin{smallmatrix}l&&\\
&h&\\
&&I_{3}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}l^{-1}I_{3}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\;l\in\mathbf{GL}_{3}(F),\;m\in F^{\times},\;h\in\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(F)\right\}\cdot\left(N_{{(1,2)}}(F)\times N_{(3,2)}(F)\right).

(3)

\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}}(q_{3})=\left\{h\times\left(\begin{smallmatrix}l&&\\
&h&\\
&&I_{2}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}l^{-1}I_{2}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\;l\in\mathbf{GL}_{2},h\in\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\right\}\cdot(\{I_{4}\}\times N_{(2,4)})
.

Proof.

Note that
Q_{(6,0)}^{\circ}(F)\setminus\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{6}}(F)
is isomorphic to the set of flags

\mathcal{V}:=\{S\subset\mathbb{H}^{6}\,:\,\mathrm{dim}(S)=6,\;S\;\text{isotropic}\}.

Let us factor
\mathbb{H}^{6}=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}:=\mathbb{H}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{4}
according to the embedding
\kappa
. Since all the quadratic spaces considered in this Proposition are hyperbolic planes, we can use
[
26
, §5]
to compute the desired double quotient. Given a subspace
S\subset\mathbb{H}^{6}
, we denote

\mathrm{rad}(S):=\{s\in S,\;s\in S^{\perp}\},

and by
P_{V_{i}}(S)=(S\times V_{j})\cap V_{i}
the projection to the space
V_{i}
with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{H}^{6}=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
. In
[
26
, §5]
it is shown that the
\kappa(\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\times\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}})(F)
-orbits on
\mathcal{V}
are uniquely determined by the following tuples:

\left(\mathrm{dim}(S\cap V_{1}),\mathrm{dim}(S\cap V_{2}),\mathrm{dim}\frac{\mathrm{rad}(P_{V_{2}}(S))}{S\cap V_{2}},b_{V_{2}}(S),b_{V_{1}}(S)\right),

where
b_{V_{2}}(S)
is the dimension of the maximal positive definite subspace of
P_{V_{2}}(S)
and
b_{V_{1}}(S)
is the dimension of the maximal negative definite subspace of
P_{V_{2}}(S)
. According to
[
26
, Theorem 5.2]
, the only possible tuples that appear are
(2,4,0,0,0),(1,3,0,1,1),(0,2,0,2,2)
. The calculation of the stabilizer of each orbit is given in
[
26
, §2.3]
. We then choose representatives
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
and
q_{3}
, respectively, as in
loc.cit.

∎

Remark 7.19
.

A representative of the open orbit is given by

q_{3}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{2}&0&0\\
0&A&0\\
0&0&I_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\text{ where }A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{2}&0_{2}&-\frac{1}{2}I_{2}&0_{2}\\
I_{2}&0_{2}&\frac{1}{2}I_{2}&0_{2}\\
0_{2}&I_{2}&0_{2}&\frac{1}{2}I_{2}\\
0_{2}&-I_{2}&0_{2}&\frac{1}{2}I_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Using Proposition
7.18
and the cuspidality of
\Psi^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}
, we proceed exactly as in Theorem
5.2
, to reduce (
58
) to the integral

(59)

\sum_{\gamma\in Q_{(2,4)}(F)\setminus\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}(F)}\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)})\Phi_{s}(q_{3}\kappa(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)},\gamma x))dh.

This integral converges for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>>0
. Consider the normalized induction
7
7

7

The modulus character of
Q_{(2,4)}
is exactly
\delta_{Q_{(2,4)}}(m(l,h))=|\mathrm{det}(l)|_{{\bf A}}^{5}|\nu^{\prime}(h)|_{{\bf A}}^{-5}
for
l\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}
,
h\in{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
.

(60)

I_{Q_{(2,4)}}(s,\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma):=\mathrm{Ind}_{Q_{(2,4)}({\bf A})}^{\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}({\bf A})}\left(|\mathrm{det}|_{{\bf A}}^{s}|\nu^{\prime}|^{-s}_{{\bf A}}(\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)\right).

For any
\varrho_{s}\in I_{Q_{(2,4)}}(s,\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)
define the Eisenstein series

E_{Q_{(2,4)}}(x,\varrho_{s}):=\sum_{\gamma\in Q_{(2,4)}(F)\setminus{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}(F)}\varrho_{s}(\gamma x),

which converges for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>>0
and admits meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane
{\bf C}
.

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
\sigma\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{2}])
,
\Psi^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime\prime}
be cusp forms in the space of
\sigma
, and let
\Phi_{s}\in I_{Q_{(6,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1})
be a holomorphic section.

(1)

For
\mathrm{Re}(s)>>0
, we have

\displaystyle q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(x):=\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)})\Phi_{s}(q_{3}\kappa(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)},x))dh\in I_{Q_{(2,4)}}(s,\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma).

(2)

We obtain the pullback formula

\int_{[\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}]}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)})\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(6,0)}}(\kappa(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(x)},x),\Phi_{s})dh=\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(2,4)}}(x,q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})).

(3)

Let
S
be a finite set of places containing the archimedean places and the ramified places of
\sigma
and
\Phi_{s}
. If
\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}\in\sigma\boxtimes\overline{\sigma}
and
\Phi_{s}=\otimes_{v}\Phi_{s,v}\in I_{Q_{(6,0)}}(s,\mathbf{1})
is normalized so that
\Phi_{s,v}(1)=1
when
v\not\in S
, we have

q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(I_{8})=\frac{L^{S}(5/2+s,\sigma\boxtimes\overline{\sigma},\mathrm{std})}{\zeta^{S}(5+2s)\zeta^{S}(3+2s)}\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A}_{S})}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(h)\Phi_{s,S}(q_{3}\kappa(h,I_{8}))dh,

where
{\bf A}_{S}=\prod_{v\in S}F_{v}
and
\Phi_{s,S}=\otimes_{v\in S}\Phi_{s,v}
. In particular
s\mapsto q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})
admits meromorphic continuation to all
s\in{\bf C}
. Hence,
q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})\in I_{Q_{(2,4)}}(s,\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)
for every
s
at which it is holomorphic.

Proof.

(1)

The invariance of
q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})
by
N_{(2,4)}({\bf A})
follows from the fact that
q_{3}\kappa(I_{4},n)q_{3}^{-1}\in N_{(6,0)}({\bf A})
.
If
m(l,h^{\prime})\in M_{(2,4)}({\bf A})\simeq\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf A})\times{\mathbf{GSO}}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})
, then
q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(m(l,h^{\prime}))
equals

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})})\Phi_{s}(q_{3}\kappa(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})},m(l,h^{\prime})))dh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})})\Phi_{s}(q_{3}\kappa(\tilde{h}^{\prime},m(l,\tilde{h}^{\prime}))\kappa({\tilde{h}}^{{}^{\prime}-1}ht_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})},m(I_{2},t_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})})))dh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathrm{det}(l)|^{5/2+s}_{{\bf A}}\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(ht_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})})\Phi_{s}(q_{3}\kappa({\tilde{h}}^{{}^{\prime}-1}ht_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})},m(I_{2},t_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})})))dh,

where in the first equality we have denoted
\tilde{h}^{\prime}:=t_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})}^{-1}h^{\prime}
, and for the second we have used that

q_{3}\kappa(\tilde{h}^{\prime},m(l,\tilde{h}^{\prime}))q_{3}^{-1}\in Q_{(6,0)}({\bf A}),

and

\delta_{Q_{(6,0)}}^{\frac{s}{5}+\frac{1}{2}}(q_{3}\kappa(\tilde{h}^{\prime},m(l,\tilde{h}^{\prime}))q_{3}^{-1})=|\mathrm{det}(l)|^{5/2+s}_{{\bf A}}.

Similarly, a direct computation shows that

\delta_{Q_{(6,0)}}^{\frac{s}{5}+\frac{1}{2}}(q_{3}\kappa(t_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})},m(I_{2},t_{\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})}))q_{3}^{-1})=|\nu^{\prime}(h^{\prime})|^{-s-5/2}.

Using this, together with the change of variables
\tilde{h}^{{}^{\prime}-1}h\mapsto h
, we obtain

(61)

\displaystyle q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(m(l,h^{\prime}))=|\mathrm{det}(l)|^{s+5/2}_{{\bf A}}|\nu(h^{\prime})|^{-s-5/2}_{{\bf A}}\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}({\bf A})}(\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime})(h^{\prime}h)\Phi_{s}(q_{3}\kappa(h,I_{8}))dh.

Hence, for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>>0
, we have
q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})\in I_{Q_{(2,4)}}(s,\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)
.

(2)

Proceeding exactly as in Theorem
5.2
, tt follows from (
59
) and part (1) above.

(3)

Assume
v
is a finite place at which both
(\sigma\boxtimes\overline{\sigma})_{v}
and
\Phi_{s,v}
are unramified. Let
v_{0}\in(\sigma\boxtimes\overline{\sigma})_{v}
be the spherical vector satisfying
\langle v_{0},v_{0}\rangle=1
. Since the space of spherical vectors of
(\sigma\boxtimes\overline{\sigma})_{v}
is one-dimensional, we have

\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(F_{v})}\Phi_{s,v}(q_{3}\kappa(h,I_{8}))(\sigma\boxtimes\overline{\sigma})_{v}(h)v_{0}dh=c_{v}(s)v_{0},

where

c_{v}(s)=\int_{\mathbf{SO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(F_{v})}\Phi_{s,v}(q_{3}\kappa(h,I_{8}))\langle(\sigma\boxtimes\overline{\sigma})_{v}(h)v_{0},v_{0}\rangle dh.

Using the Cartan decomposition as in Theorem
5.2
(2) and
[
19
, Proposition 6.4 (a) & Proof of Theorem 6.1]
, one shows that
c_{v}(s)
equals

\frac{L(5/2+s,\sigma_{v}\boxtimes\overline{\sigma}_{v},\mathrm{std})}{\zeta_{v}(5+2s)\zeta_{v}(3+2s)}.

∎

Denote

(62)

\displaystyle e:\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}},

\displaystyle(r,a)

\displaystyle\mapsto e(r,a):=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}r&\\
&aI_{4}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}r^{-1}I_{4}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Corollary 7.21
.

Assume (
(Spl)
) with
\lambda_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})^{\sim}=\phi_{\frac{1}{2}},
for
f_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A}))
and
f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}({\bf A})^{2})
. Let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
have support outside
0
. We have

J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)=\tfrac{\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}}{4}\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{1}\backslash(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1})]}|a|_{\bf A}^{-2z}|\mathrm{det}(r)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\varphi(r)\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(2,4)}}(e(r,a),q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{-\frac{1}{2}}))drda.

Proof.

It follows from Propositions
7.17
and
7.20
, that, for
{\rm Re}(z)>>0
, we have

J(\tfrac{1}{2},z,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)=\tfrac{\zeta^{S}_{F}(2)^{2}}{4}\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{1}\backslash(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1})]}|a|_{\bf A}^{-2z}|\mathrm{det}(r)|^{z}_{{\bf A}}\varphi(r)\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(2,4)}}(e(r,a),q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{-\frac{1}{2}}))drda.

Since the left hand side defines a holomorphic function on
z
and the right hand side gives a holomorphic function on
z
because of rapid decay of
\varphi
, we get the desired equality.
∎

7.7.
The spherical period

Consider the embedding of algebraic groups

\displaystyle\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2},

\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},g_{2}),g_{2}\right),

where
\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},g_{2})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}&\\
&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. The quotient

\mathbf{X}:=(\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})\setminus(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}),

defines a strongly tempered spherical variety (see
[
55
, Table 1]
). For
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{4}])
and
\Psi\in\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{2}])
with trivial product of central characters, define

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}(\varphi,\Psi):=\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\varphi(\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},g_{2}))\Psi(g_{2})dg_{1}dg_{2}.

This is the automorphic period attached to the spherical variety
\mathbf{X}
(or rather to the BZSV quadruple
(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2},\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2},0,1)
in the notation of
[
40
]
). Associated with
\mathbf{X}
, there is also an automorphic
L
-function
L(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})
on
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
(see
[
49
]
and
[
55
]
), where
\rho_{\mathbf{X}}
is the representation of
{}^{L}(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})
, given by

\rho_{\mathbf{X}}:=\wedge^{2}\otimes{\rm std}_{2}\oplus{\rm std}_{4}\oplus{\rm std}_{4}^{\vee},

where recall that
{\rm std}_{n}
and
{\rm std}_{n}^{\vee}
denote the standard representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{n}({\bf C})
and its dual, respectively.

The aim of this section is to set Conjecture
1.1
, when
\pi\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{4}])
and
\sigma\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rm cusp}([\mathbf{GL}_{2}])
are unramified everywhere. In particular, we will show that

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0\Longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

Note that the direction
\Longleftarrow
follows by
[
44
, Corollary 1.3(6)]
and
[
56
, Theorem 1.1]
, under certain additional hypotheses
8
8

8

The work
[
44
]
assumes that
\pi
and
\sigma
have trivial central characters and that
L(\frac{3}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0
, which is always true if
\pi
and
\sigma
are tempered. Under these hypotheses, they deduce that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0\Longrightarrow L(\frac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0
. To obtain the non-vanishing of
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})
, one also needs a comparison between
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}
and generalized Shalika periods for
\Theta^{0}(\pi)\otimes\sigma
, which we discuss in Lemma
7.30
.

To this end, our aim is to relate the period
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}
to

(63)

\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{1}\backslash(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1})]}\varphi(r)\mathrm{E}_{Q_{(2,4)}}(e(r,a),q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s}))drda,

as it plays a key role in proving that

J(\tfrac{1}{2},0,\varphi,f^{\Psi}_{\phi_{1/2}},\eta)\neq 0\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
Q_{(2,4)}
be the parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}
with Levi component equal to
\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
. We have that

Q_{(2,4)}(F)\setminus\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}(F)/e(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1})(F)=\{\omega_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,7}.

For
1\leq i\leq 6
, the stabilizer of
\omega_{i}
in
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
contains a normal unipotent subgroup. Moreover, the stabilizer of the (open) orbit
\omega_{7}
is isomorphic to

\left\{\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}&\\
&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\mathrm{det}(g_{1})\right),\;g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F)\right\}\subset\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F).

Proof.

The quotient
Q_{(2,4)}(F)\setminus\mathbf{GSO}_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}(F)
can be identified with the set of flags

\mathcal{V}:=\{S\subset\mathbb{H}^{4},\;\mathrm{dim}(S)=2,\;S\;\text{isotropic}\}.

Set
U_{1}:=\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
and
U_{2}:=\langle f_{4},f_{3},f_{2},f_{1}\rangle
. Given
S\in\mathcal{V}
, consider the following invariants for the
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F)
-orbit
\mathcal{S}
of
S
:

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{S}}:=(\mathrm{dim}(S\cap U_{1}),\mathrm{dim}(S\cap U_{2})).

When
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq(0,0)
, we get 5 (closed) orbits corresponding to
(2,0),(0,2),(1,1),(1,0),(0,1)
. An elementary computation shows that the stabilizer of each of these orbits contains a normal unipotent subgroup. We omit the details. Suppose that
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{S}}=(0,0)
. For
S\in\mathcal{S}
, write

(64)

S=\langle x,y\rangle=\langle p_{U_{1}}(x)+p_{U_{2}}(x),p_{U_{1}}(y)+p_{U_{2}}(y)\rangle,

where
p_{U_{i}}(\cdot)
is the projection map to the vector space
U_{i}
. Any element
(g,\lambda)\in\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F)
acts on
S
via

\langle x,y\rangle\cdot e(g,\lambda)=\langle p_{U_{1}}(x)\cdot g+p_{U_{2}}(x)\cdot\lambda g^{\prime},p_{U_{1}}(y)\cdot g+p_{U_{2}}(y)\cdot\lambda g^{\prime}\rangle,

where
g^{\prime}=I_{4}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}g^{-1}I_{4}^{\prime}
. After using the
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)
-action, we can suppose that
p_{U_{1}}(x)=e_{1}
and
p_{U_{1}}(y)=e_{2}
. We now show there are two
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F)
-orbit
\mathcal{S}
of planes satisfying
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{S}}=(0,0)
with representatives equal to
\langle e_{1}+f_{3},e_{2}+f_{4}\rangle
and
\langle e_{1}-f_{2},e_{2}+f_{1}\rangle
. Indeed, if
S=\langle e_{1}+p_{U_{2}}(x),e_{2}+p_{U_{2}}(y)\rangle
, we can assume (because of isotropy of
S
)

\displaystyle p_{U_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{2}f_{2}+\alpha_{3}f_{3}+\alpha_{4}f_{4}

\displaystyle p_{U_{2}}(y)

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{2}f_{1}+\beta_{3}f_{3}+\beta_{4}f_{4},

with
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in F
.

•

Suppose that
\alpha_{2}=0
. Then, the pair
\{p_{U_{2}}(x),p_{U_{2}}(y)\}
defines a basis of
\langle f_{3},f_{4}\rangle
. Using the action of the Levi of the Siegel parabolic
P
of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)
, which stabilizes
9
9

9

As we are letting
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
act on the right, this Siegel parabolic is contained in the lower triangular Borel of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
the plane
\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle
, we can thus send
\langle e_{1}+p_{U_{2}}(x),e_{2}+p_{U_{2}}(y)\rangle
to
\langle e_{1}+f_{3},e_{2}+f_{4}\rangle
. A direct computation shows that
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F)}(\omega_{6})
contains, as a normal subgroup, the unipotent radical of
P
.

•

Suppose that
\alpha_{2}\neq 0
. Use the action of the unipotent radical of
P
to send
\langle e_{1}+p_{U_{2}}(x),e_{2}+p_{U_{2}}(y)\rangle
to
\langle e_{1}+\alpha_{2}f_{2},e_{2}-\alpha_{2}f_{1}\rangle
. Finally, the action of
e(\{I_{4}\}\times F^{\times})
let us send
S
to
\omega_{7}:=\langle e_{1}-f_{2},e_{2}+f_{1}\rangle
. By direct computation, its stabilizer is given by

\left\{\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}&\\
&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\mathrm{det}(g_{1})\right),\;g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F)\right\}\subset\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}(F).

∎

Remark 7.23
.

In matrix form, the representative of the open orbit can be chosen to be

\omega_{7}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{2}&&-A\\
&I_{4}&\\
A&&0_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\text{ with }A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Note that the section
q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})
gives a family of cusp forms on
\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma
. To simplify exposition, we give the following.

Notation 7.24
.

For any
(r,a)\in\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}({\bf A})
and
s\in{\bf C}
, denote by

(\Lambda_{r,a,s}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Lambda^{\prime\prime}_{r,a,s})(h):=q_{(\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)(h^{-1})\cdot\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}e(r,a)).

Concretely,
\Lambda_{r,a,s}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Lambda^{\prime\prime}_{r,a,s}
is a cusp form of
\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma
, depending, in particular, on the section
\Phi_{s}
and the cusp form
\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}
of
\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma
.

Proposition 7.25
.

When
\mathrm{Re}(s)>>0
, the integral (
63
) equals

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Y}({\bf A})^{\circ}}\Lambda_{r,a,s}^{\prime}(I_{2})\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\varphi({\rm diag}(g_{1},g_{2})r)\Lambda^{\prime\prime}_{r,a,s}(g_{2})dg_{1}dg_{2}drda.

where
\mathbf{Y}({\bf A})^{\circ}:=(\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})({\bf A})\backslash(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1})({\bf A})
.

Proof.

We proceed as in Theorem
6.9
by unfolding the integral (
63
). For
{\rm Re}(s)
big enough, the Eisenstein series unfolds and, by Lemma
7.22
and the cuspidality of
\varphi
and
\Psi^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}
, (
63
) is shown to be equal to

(65)

\int_{(\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2})(F)\mathbf{GL}_{1}({\bf A})\backslash(\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1})({\bf A})}\varphi(r)q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}e(r,a))drda,

where we recall that
\mathbf{GL}_{1}
embeds into
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{1}
via (
57
). Collapsing the integral over
[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]
, we get

(66)

\int_{\mathbf{Y}({\bf A})^{\circ}}\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\varphi({\rm diag}(g_{1},g_{2})r)q_{\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}e({\rm diag}(g_{1},g_{2})r,\mathrm{det}(g_{1})a))drda.

Consider the element

\alpha:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&&&&\\
&\mathrm{det}(g_{1})^{-1}\mathrm{det}(g_{2})&&&&\\
&&I_{2}&&&\\
&&&\mathrm{det}(g_{1})^{-1}\mathrm{det}(g_{2})I_{2}&&\\
&&&&1&\\
&&&&&\mathrm{det}(g_{1})^{-1}\mathrm{det}(g_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in Q_{(2,4)}({\bf A}),

which commutes with
\omega_{7}
. Note

\omega_{7}e(\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mathrm{det}(g_{1}))\alpha\omega_{7}^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathrm{det}(g_{2})A^{\prime}\;{}^{t}(g_{1}\cdot t_{g_{1},g_{2}})^{-1}A^{\prime-1}&&&\star\\
&g_{2}&&\\
&&\mathrm{det}(g_{2})I_{2}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}g_{2}^{-1}I_{2}^{\prime}&\\
&&&Ag_{1}\cdot t_{g_{1},g_{2}}A^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in Q_{(2,4)}({\bf A}),

where
A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\\
&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
A^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and
t_{g_{1},g_{2}}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\\
&\mathrm{det}(g_{1})^{-1}\mathrm{det}(g_{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Thus, multiplying and dividing by
\alpha
to the right of
\omega_{7}e(\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mathrm{det}(g_{1}))\omega_{7}^{-1}
, by Proposition
7.20
(1), we have that the integral (
66
) equals

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Y}({\bf A})^{\circ}}\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\varphi({\rm diag}(g_{1},g_{2})r)q_{(\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)(\tilde{g}_{2}^{-1})\cdot\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}\alpha^{-1}e(r,a))dg_{1}dg_{2}drda,

where we denoted
\tilde{g}_{2}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&\\
&\mathrm{det}(g_{2})I_{2}^{\prime}\;{}^{t}g_{2}^{-1}I_{2}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Since
\alpha^{-1}=e\left(m_{g_{1},g_{2}},\mathrm{det}(g_{1})\mathrm{det}(g_{2})^{-1}\right)
, where

m_{g_{1},g_{2}}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&&\\
&\mathrm{det}(g_{1})\mathrm{det}(g_{2})^{-1}&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A}),

we do the change of variables

\mathrm{det}(g_{1})\mathrm{det}(g_{2})^{-1}a\mapsto a,\;\;\;\;m_{g_{1},g_{2}}r\mapsto r,

to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Y}({\bf A})^{\circ}}\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\varphi({\rm diag}(g_{1},g_{2})m_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{-1}r)q_{(\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)(\tilde{g}_{2}^{-1})\cdot\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}e(r,a))dg_{1}dg_{2}drda

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Y}({\bf A})^{\circ}}\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\varphi({\rm diag}(g_{1}t_{g_{1},g_{2}},g_{2})r)q_{(\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)(\tilde{g}_{2}^{-1})\cdot\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}e(r,a))dg_{1}dg_{2}drda.

Lastly, we do the change of variables
g_{1}t_{g_{1},g_{2}}\mapsto g_{1}
, so that the above integral equals

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Y}({\bf A})^{\circ}}\int_{Z_{\mathbf{GL}_{4}}({\bf A})\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\!\!\!\!\varphi({\rm diag}(g_{1},g_{2})r)q_{(\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)(\tilde{g}_{2}^{-1})\cdot\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}e(r,a))dg_{1}dg_{2}drda.

The result now follows from the fact that, via the exceptional isomorphism,
\tilde{g}_{2}
corresponds to
(I_{2},g_{2})
(see Remark
2.4
). Then

\displaystyle q_{(\overline{\sigma}\boxtimes\sigma)(\tilde{g}_{2}^{-1})\cdot\overline{\Psi}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Psi^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{s})(\omega_{7}e(r,a))=\left(\Lambda_{r,a,s}^{\prime}\boxtimes\Lambda^{\prime\prime}_{r,a,s}\right)(\tilde{g_{2}})=\Lambda_{r,a,s}^{\prime}(I_{2})\Lambda^{\prime\prime}_{r,a,s}(g_{2}),

obtaining the result.
∎

Theorem 7.26
.

Let
\pi
and
\sigma
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
respectively, such that
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=\mathbf{1}.

Assume that
\phi_{1/2}=\otimes_{v}\phi_{1/2,v}\in I_{P_{(3,0)}}(1/2,\mathbf{1})
satisfies the hypothesis (
(Spl)
). Then, for any cusp form
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}\in\pi
and Schwartz function
\eta=\prod_{v}\eta_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
with support outside
0
and unramified outside
S
, we have that if

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L^{S}(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\prod_{v\in S}J^{\star}(1/2,0,\varphi_{v},f_{\phi_{1/2,v}}^{\Psi},\eta_{v})\neq 0,

there exists a cusp form
\Lambda\in\sigma
, depending on
f
and
\eta
, such that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}(\varphi,\Lambda)\neq 0
.

Proof.

It follows from Corollary
7.6
, Corollary
7.21
, and Proposition
7.25
.
∎

Corollary 7.27
.

Let
\pi
and
\sigma
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
and
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
respectively, such that
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=\mathbf{1}
.

(1)

Assume that there exist
\phi\in\Pi(\mathbb{H}^{2})
,
\eta=\otimes_{v}\eta_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
,
\Psi=\otimes_{v}\Psi_{v}\in\sigma
, and
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}\in\pi
such that

\prod_{v\in S}J^{\star}(1/2,0,\varphi_{v},f^{\Psi}_{\phi,v},\eta_{v})\neq 0.

Then

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4})L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4}^{\vee})L^{S}(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

(2)

If
\pi
and
\sigma
are unramified everywhere, we have

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

Proof.

We first show that the non-vanishing of
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4}^{\vee})
is equivalent to the non-vanishing of
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})
. For any place
v
, we denote by
\phi_{\pi_{v}}
the Langland parameter of the representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v})
. By the local Langlands correspondence for
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
, we have that
\phi_{\pi_{v}^{\vee}}=\phi_{\pi_{v}}^{\vee}
(see
[
1
, (1.4)]
), which implies that

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi^{\vee},{\rm std}_{4})=L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4}^{\vee}).

Moreover, the functional equation gives

L(s,\pi,{\rm std}_{4})=\varepsilon(s,\pi,{\rm std}_{4})L(1-s,\pi^{\vee},{\rm std}_{4}).

Consequently,
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4})\neq 0
if and only if
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,{\rm std}_{4}^{\vee})\neq 0
. Then, (1) follows from Theorem
7.26
. For (2),
recall that, thanks to the archimedean computation of Theorem
4.1
, Corollary
7.6
shows that there exist data
(\varphi,f,\eta)
such that
J(\varphi,f,\eta,s,z)
calculates the product of completed
L
-functions

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4})L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2}).

Since
S=\emptyset
, Lemma
6.10
(ii) allows us to choose a non-trivial section
f=f^{\Psi}_{\phi}
, which is unramified at every place. Then, the implication follows from Theorem
7.26
.
∎

Remark 7.28
.

We expect the hypothesis on the non-vanishing of the local zeta integrals at any place
v
in
S
to be related to the non-vanishing of the local relative characters for the strongly tempered pair
(\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F_{v}),\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v})\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F_{v}))
. For example, an instance of this phenomenon is given in
[
28
]
(see the proof of Theorem 1.1 and equation (5.1) of
loc.cit.
).

7.8.
The relation between generalized Shalika periods and linear periods

In what follows, we conclude the proof of Theorem
B
. Recall that, in
[
44
]
, under certain hypotheses on
\pi\otimes\sigma
, it is shown that the non-vanishing of the period over
\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
implies that the central
L
-value
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0
. To further show that this implies the non-vanishing of
L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})
, we use the fact that the non-vanishing of
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}
implies that
\pi
is in the image of the theta correspondence from
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
. The latter essentially follows from the computations of
[
16
, §3]
and from the proof of
[
9
, Proposition 7.1]
.

Consider the embedding

\displaystyle m:\mathbf{GL}_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{GL}_{4},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(g,g)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g&\\
&g\end{smallmatrix}\right).

We also let
N
denote the unipotent radical of the Siegel parabolic of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
, defined by

N(R)=\left\{n(A):=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{2}&A\\
&I_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\,\text{with }A\in M_{2}(R)\right\},

for any
F
-algebra
R
. Observe that
\mathbf{GL}_{2}
embeds via
m
into the Levi of the Siegel parabolic, thus acts by conjugation on
N
. The Shalika subgroup of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
is given by
S:=\mathbf{GL}_{2}\,N
. Fix the character
\psi_{S}:[S]\to{\bf C}^{\times}
, given by

m(g)\,n(A)\mapsto\psi\left({\rm Tr}(A)\right).

Definition 7.29
.

Given cusp forms
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{{\rm cusp}}(\mathbf{GL}_{4})
,
\Psi\in\mathcal{A}_{{\rm cusp}}(\mathbf{GL}_{2})
, define the generalized Shalika period by

\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}({\varphi,\Psi}):=\int_{{\bf A}^{\times}\backslash[\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\int_{[N]}\varphi(n\,m(g))\Psi(g)\psi_{S}^{-1}(n)dn\,dg.

The authors thank Wee Teck Gan for sharing the following result with us.

Lemma 7.30
.

Given a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi\otimes\sigma
of
\mathbf{GL}_{4}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}
, we have

\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}|_{\Theta^{0}(\pi)\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0\Longleftrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

In particular, if
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0
, then
\pi
is in the image of the theta correspondence from
\mathbf{GL}_{4}
.

Proof.

The proof follows closely that of
[
9
, Proposition 7.1]
. For the reader’s convenience, we sketch it.

Let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(M_{4}({\bf A}))
be a Schwartz function whose support does not contain zero. For
\mathrm{Re}(z)>>0
, consider

\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}({\varphi_{\eta}^{z},\Psi})=\int_{{\bf A}^{\times}\backslash[S]}\Psi(g)\psi_{S}^{-1}(n)\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}|\mathrm{det}(h)|^{z}\varphi(h)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\end{subarray}}(\omega_{4,4}(n\,m(g),h)\eta)(x)dh\,dn\,dg.

Choose two
4
-dimensional
F
vector spaces
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
with
M_{4}(F)\simeq L_{1}^{\vee}\otimes L_{2}
and fix a decomposition
L_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}
, where
\mathrm{dim}_{F}\;\mathcal{L}_{i}=2
. Let

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}(M_{4}({\bf A}))\to\mathcal{S}(L_{1}({\bf A})\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\vee}({\bf A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(L_{1}^{\vee}({\bf A})\otimes\mathcal{L}_{2}({\bf A}))

be the partial Fourier transform of
[
16
, §3.2]
. By Poisson summation

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in M_{4}(F)\end{subarray}}(\omega_{4,4}(h_{1},h_{2})\eta)(x)=\sum_{(y,t)\in(L_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\vee})\oplus(L_{1}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{2})}(\omega(h_{1},h_{2})\mathcal{F}(\eta))(y,t),

with
\omega
denoting the action of the Weil representation on this different model. Using the explicit action of
N
on this model, as given by the formulas in
loc.cit.
, the inner integral over
[N]
of
\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}({\varphi_{\eta}^{z},\Psi})
is non-zero for
(y,t)
satisfying
ty=I_{4}
, while it vanishes otherwise for orthogonality of characters. Hence,

\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}({\varphi_{\eta}^{z},\Psi})=\int_{{\bf A}^{\times}\setminus[\mathbf{GL}_{2}]}\Psi(g)\int_{[\mathbf{GL}_{4}]}|\mathrm{det}(h)|^{z}\varphi(h)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,t)\in\mathcal{O}\end{subarray}}(\omega(m(g),h)\mathcal{F}(\eta))(y,t)dhdg,

where
\mathcal{O}:=\{(y,t)\in(L_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\vee})\oplus(L_{1}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{2}),\,\text{with }\,ty=I_{4}\}
. By
[
16
, §3.3]
, the group
\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F)
acts transitively on
\mathcal{O}
. Furthermore, as explained in
loc.cit.
, one can choose
(y_{\mathcal{O}},t_{\mathcal{O}})\in\mathcal{O}
such that its stabilizer is

H(F)=\left\{(g,\mathrm{diag}(g,h))\,:\,g,h\in\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F)\right\}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{GL}_{2}(F)\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}(F).

Unfolding the sum yields that
\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}({\varphi_{\eta}^{z},\Psi})
equals

(67)

\displaystyle\int_{H({\bf A})\setminus(\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf A})\times\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})}(\omega(m(g),h)\mathcal{F}(\eta))(y_{\mathcal{O}},t_{\mathcal{O}})\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}(\pi(h)(\varphi\otimes|\mathrm{det}(-)|^{z}),\sigma(g)\Psi)dhdg.

Note that one can choose
\eta
so that the integral (
67
) converges absolutely when
z=0
, which shows that

\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}|_{\Theta^{0}(\pi)\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

Finally, arguing as in
[
9
, Proposition 7.1]
, one can further choose
\eta
so that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}(\varphi,\Psi)\neq 0
implies that the integral (
67
) is non-zero at
z=0
. Hence,
\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{S}}|_{\Theta^{0}(\pi)\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0
as desired.
∎

Thanks to Lemma
7.30
, using a combination of the above-mentioned result of
[
44
]
and Corollary
7.27
, we can prove
[
55
, Conjecture 1.9]
for Model 1 of
loc.cit.
in the case where
\pi
and
\sigma
are unramified everywhere.

Corollary 7.31
.

Let
\pi
and
\sigma
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{PGL}_{4}
and
\mathbf{PGL}_{2}
, which are unramified everywhere. Suppose that
L(\frac{3}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0
. Then,

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\rho_{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0\Longleftrightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}}|_{\pi\otimes\sigma}\not\equiv 0.

Proof.

The implication
(\Longrightarrow)
follows from Corollary
7.27
(2).

Now, assume first that there exist cusp forms
\varphi,\Psi
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{GL}_{2}\times\mathbf{GL}_{2}}(\varphi,\Psi)\neq 0
. By
[
44
, Corollary 1.3(6)]
, we have

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})\neq 0.

Moreover, by Lemma
7.30
, we have that
\Theta^{0}(\pi)\neq 0
. Thus, Proposition
7.1
, together with the discussion in Corollary
7.27
, implies that

L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4})L(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi,\mathrm{std}_{4}^{\vee})\neq 0,

which proves the implication
(\Longleftarrow)
.
∎

8.
The pole of the degree 12
L
-function

Let
\pi
be a globally generic cuspidal automorphic representation of either
\mathbf{GL}_{4}({\bf A})
or
{\mathbf{GU}}_{2,2}({\bf A})
and let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{GL}_{2}({\bf A})
. We suppose that both representations are unramified outside a finite set
S
of places of
F
. In this section, we study the analytic properties of the partial
L
-function
L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
. We start with the following.

Proposition 8.1
.

The
L
-function
L^{S}(s,\pi\otimes\sigma,\wedge^{2}\otimes\mathrm{std}_{2})
is entire unless
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}\neq 1
and
(\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma})^{2}=1
. In this case, it can have at most a simple pole at
s=1
.

Proof.

The proof relies on the analysis of the poles of the zeta integral
Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})
. As we discussed in §
3.1
, the Klingen Eisenstein series
E^{*}_{P_{(1,2)}}(g,s,f_{s})
is entire unless
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}\neq 1
and
(\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma})^{2}=1
, in which case it can have a simple pole at
s=1
. Therefore,
Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})
can only have a pole at
s=1
provided that
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}\neq 1
and
(\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma})^{2}=1
. The result then follows from Theorem
3.5
and Proposition
3.11
.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

We note that Proposition
8.1
can be equivalently proved by using the pullback formula (see Corollary
5.3
) and analyzing the zeta integral at points where the normalized Siegel Eisenstein series
E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(g,s,\phi_{s})
can have poles. As a sanity check, we provide a sketch of the proof using this alternative method. It is well known that
E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(g,s,\phi_{s})
is entire if
(\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma})^{2}\neq 1
and it has simple poles at
s=1
, resp.
s=1
and
2
, when
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}\neq 1
and
(\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma})^{2}=1
, resp.
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=1
. We now explain how to deduce in the latter case that the zeta integral is entire everywhere using Corollary
5.3
. Assume
\omega_{\pi}\omega_{\sigma}=1
; the residue at
s=2
of
E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(g,s,\phi_{s})
is constant, hence the residue at
s=2
of the right hand side of (
31
), hence of
Z(s,\varphi,f_{s})
, is zero because the period of the cusp form
\varphi
over
{\mathbf{Sp}}_{4}
is zero. At
s=1
, the residue of
E_{P_{(3,0)}}^{*}(g,s,\phi_{s})
is proportional to a value of a degenerate Klingen Eisenstein series
E_{P_{(1,4)}}(g,s_{0},\phi_{s_{0}}^{\prime})
(see
[
29
, Proposition 1.10]
), where
P_{(1,4)}
is the standard parabolic of
{\mathbf{GSp}}_{6}
with Levi
\mathbf{GL}_{1}\times{\mathbf{GSp}}_{4}
, for some
s_{0}\in{\bf C}
and section
\phi_{s_{0}}^{\prime}
. Then the residue of (
31
) at
s=1
is zero because a simple unfolding argument shows that
